Structures on the category of N N -complexes
Felix Küng

Abstract




Abstract

The theory of
N
-complexes is a generalization of both ordinary chain complexes and graded objects. Hence it yields deeper insight in the structure of these and offers a broader range of applications.
This work generalizes the tensor product of chain complexes and graded objects to the case of
N
-complexes using the structures of
q
-binomial coefficients. We then study different approaches to realize the derived category of
N
-complexes. In particular we realize it as the Verdier quotient of the homotopy category of
N
-complexes, as the
\mathrm{h}
-projective objects and as the homotopy category of a category admitting a Quillen model structure.

Introduction

The theory of chain complexes is a very thoroughly studied field in mathematics. Since a defining property of these is
d^{2}=0
, an obvious generalization is the notion of an
N
-complex, which has the same structure as a chain complex but instead of
d^{2}=0
we have
d^{N}=0
.

Objects of this form were first studied by W. Mayer
[
17
]
in 1942 as an alternative homology theory on topological spaces. This approach did not get significant attention since this
N
-homology theory fulfills the Eilenberg-Steenrod axioms and therefore has to be equivalent to ordinary homology. Later in 1991 Kapranov studied them from a purely algebraic point of view
[
13
]
which spiked great interest in that topic. In particular a considerable amount of homological framework for these was developed by M. Dubois-Violette
[
6
,
7
]
. One of the main motivations of these papers was that
N
-complexes can be used to study representations of quantum groups similarly to the way one uses chain complexes to study representations of groups. Furthermore the concept of cohomology of
N
-complexes also got attention in physics where it is referred to as amplitude cohomology, or homology depending on the convention
[
4
]
. After this initial work there have been papers about the relation of
N
-complexes to other topics such as Koszulity
[
2
]
, simplicial structures
[
18
,
19
]
or dualizing categories
[
9
]
. An especially interesting example of the work done in this field is the paper “Algebraic Model for
N
-connection”
[
1
]
since it applies the otherwise purely algebraic notions of
N
-complexes to geometry and suggests an application of
N
-complexes to non-flat connections. During the last few years the concept of
N
-complexes has also arrived in the field of categorification: in particular categorification at roots of unity relies on the theory of
N
-complexes. For example M. Khovanov used hopfological algebra to model
N
-complexes in his paper “Hopfological algebra and categorification at a root of unity: the first steps”
[
15
]
.

Although there was already quite some theory on the applications and properties of
N
-complexes, a rigid foundation of the homological properties and constructions used in these papers was missing. This has been remedied by Iyama, Kato and Miyachi in
[
12
]
. However, some of the notions defined in their paper deserve a more thorough study, and although one has now this solid foundation of the theory involved, there are still some open questions on the structure of
N
-complexes.

For example:

•

Can we construct a canonical monoidal structure on the category of
N
-complexes if the underlying category is monoidal?

•

Can one characterize the derived category of
N
-complexes as well over h-projectives?

•

Is the derived category really the Gabriel-Zismann localisation of the category of
N
-complexes along
N
-quasi-isomorphisms?

Throughout this work we will try to answer these questions and give very thorough study of the properties of the involved structures.

In the first three sections we will collect some general properties of
N
-complexes, study
q
-binomial coefficients and apply these to lift bifunctors using a twisted Leibniz rule. This involves the construction of certain standard
N
-complexes, proving that the category of
N
-complexes is complete, cocomplete, abelian or additive if the underlying category is, a weaker but more intuitive form of cyclic sieving and how we can lift which functors and bifunctors.

In the remaining section
4
we then apply these structures of
q
-binomial coefficients and liftable functors to lift monoidal structures in most cases.

We continue in section
5
and
6
by studying the homotopy structure of the category of
N
-complexes, we start by constructing its homotopy category as stable category of a Frobenius exact sturcture and prove that it is triangulated.

Using this structure we imitate in section
7
the three classical constructions for realizing the derived category as Verdier-quotient,
\mathrm{h}
-projective objects and prove that these can be identified by considering semifree objects as
\mathrm{h}
-projective resolutions.

Finally in section
8
we finish by defining a Quillen model structure on the category of
N
-complexes realizing the derived category as homotopy category. This shows in particular that the derived category of
N
-complexes indeed is the Gabriel-Zismann localisation along
N
-quasi-isomorphisms.

Although the model structure constructed in section
8
coincides with the model structure constructed by J. Gillespie and M. Hovey in their paper
[
8
]
, our work is completely independent and also uses different techniques that are more hands on. Furthermore it is quite natural that both approaches yield the same model structure since it is the obvious generalization of the projective model structure on the category of chain complexes.

1.
The category
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})

The aim of this section is to introduce the concept of
N
-complexes. In particular we will give the definition and first properties of the category of
N
-complexes in a pointed category. We will use a general definition of diagrams that look like
N
-complexes, without restriction on the differential, which we will refer to as
\infty
-complexes. This allows the use of some abstract arguments for the structure and existence of this category and later also simplifies certain proofs about properties of the category of
N
-complexes. After that we will define the category of
N
-complexes as a full subcategory and discuss certain “standard”
N
-complexes which resemble the standard projective chain complexes and admit similar adjunctions as in the chain-complex case. Later we will prove that the category of
N
-complexes and
\infty
-complexes inherits properties from their base category. In particular we will show that the category of
N
-complexes is abelian if the ground category is, and that it admits colimits and limits if the ground category does.

Let us begin by defining the category of
\infty
-complexes as the category of diagrams of a certain shape in an arbitrary category.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category.

(1)

An
\infty
-complex in
\mathcal{C}
is a diagram

\cdot\cdot\cdot
X^{n-1}
X^{n}
X^{n+1}
\cdot\cdot\cdot
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}

in
\mathcal{C}
.

We will sometimes drop the
\mathcal{C}
if it is clear from the context.

(2)

A
morphism
g:X\to Y
of
\infty
-complexes in
\mathcal{C}
is a family of morphisms
\left(g^{i}:X^{i}\to Y^{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
, such that the following diagram in
\mathcal{C}
commutes:

\cdot\cdot\cdot
X^{i-1}
X^{i}
X^{i+1}
\cdot\cdot\cdot
\cdot\cdot\cdot
Y^{i-1}
Y^{i}
Y^{i+1}
\cdot\cdot\cdot
.
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{Y}
d_{Y}
d_{Y}
d_{Y}
g^{i-1}
g^{i}
g^{i+1}

Lemma 1.2
.

The collection of
\infty
-complexes in
\mathcal{C}
together with morphisms of
\infty
-complexes in
\mathcal{C}
defines a category, which we will refer to as
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{I}
be the category with objects
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathrm{I}(x,y):=\{*\}
if
x\leq y
and
\mathrm{I}(x,y):=\emptyset
otherwise. Then
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
is the category of functors from
\mathrm{I}
to
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Corollary 1.3
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is pre-additive, additive or abelian, so is
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

This is clear by the identification of
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
as functors from
\mathrm{I}
to
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Now we can define the category of
N
-complexes as a full subcategory of the category of
\infty
-complexes.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pointed category, i. e. a category with a zero object, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

An
\infty
-complex
X
in
\mathcal{C}
is an
N
-complex in
\mathcal{C}
or just an
N
-complex
, if
d_{X}^{N}=0
.

(2)

We denote the full subcategory of
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
consisting of the
N
-complexes by
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
.

It is particularly interesting to observe that the notion of
N
-complexes generalizes the following cases.

Observation 1.5
.

There are three well known cases for the category of
N
-complexes in a pointed category
\mathcal{C}
:

N=0
:

In this case we have for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
that
d_{X}^{0}=\mathrm{Id}_{X^{n}}=0
, hence
X^{n}\cong 0
and so
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
is the subcategory consisting of zero objects in
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
.

N=1
:

Here the above equation yields
d_{X}=0
. Since morphisms in a pointed category always commute with the zero map we get that the category
\mathrm{C}_{1}(\mathcal{C})
is isomorphic to the category of graded objects in
\mathcal{C}
.

N=2
:

This case yields by definition the case of chain complexes in
\mathcal{C}
.

Since we will later imitate constructions from the theory of chain complexes, we will need to define the following objects introduced in
[
12
]
and prove that they give rise to certain adjunctions. This will be one of the main ingredients for our constructions regarding the homotopy category of
N
-complexes and the projective model structure.

Definition 1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pointed category.

(1)

Let
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. The diagram

\cdot\cdot\cdot
0
X
X
\cdot\cdot\cdot
X
X
0
\cdot\cdot\cdot
\cdot\cdot\cdot
i-N
i-N+1
i-N+2
\cdot\cdot\cdot
i-1
i
i+1
\cdot\cdot\cdot
.
0
0
\mathrm{Id}_{X}
\mathrm{Id}_{X}
\mathrm{Id}_{X}
\mathrm{Id}_{X}
0
0

defines an
N
-complex that we denote by
\mu^{i}_{N}(X)
. Here the numbers in the top row denote the degree of the corresponding object.

(2)

Let
g:M\to N
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then
\mu_{N}^{i}(g):\mu_{N}^{i}(M)\to\mu_{N}^{i}(N)
is the morphism of
N
-complexes in
\mathcal{C}
which is levelwise
(\mu_{N}^{i}(g))^{j}:=g
for
i-N<j\leq i
and the zero morphism otherwise.

Example 1.7
.

Consider the
\mathbb{C}
-vectorspace
\mathbb{C}
. Then we have that
\mu_{4}^{7}(\mathbb{C})
is the following
4
-complex

\cdot\cdot\cdot
0
\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
0
\cdot\cdot\cdot
\cdot\cdot\cdot
3
4
5
6
7
8
\cdot\cdot\cdot
0
0
\mathrm{Id}_{X}
\mathrm{Id}_{X}
\mathrm{Id}_{X}
0
0

in the category of
\mathbb{C}
-vectorspaces.

Proposition 1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pointed category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the assignments
M\mapsto\mu_{N}^{i}(M)
and
g\mapsto\mu_{N}^{i}(g)
define functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Furthermore

(1)

the functor
(\_)^{i}:\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
is left adjoint to
\mu_{N}^{i}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
,

(2)

the functor
\mu_{N}^{i}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
is left adjoint to
(\_)^{i-N+1}:\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

(1)

Let
X\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
and
M\in\mathcal{C}
. If
f:X^{i}\to M
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then

\cdot\cdot\cdot
X^{i-N}
X^{i-N+1}
X^{i-N+2}
\cdot\cdot\cdot
X^{i-1}
X^{i}
X^{i+1}
\cdot\cdot\cdot
\cdot\cdot\cdot
0
M
M
\cdot\cdot\cdot
M
M
0
\cdot\cdot\cdot
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
0
0
\mathrm{Id}_{M}
\mathrm{Id}_{M}
\mathrm{Id}_{M}
\mathrm{Id}_{M}
0
0
0
f\circ d^{N-1}
f\circ d^{N-2}
f\circ d
f
0

is a morphism
\widehat{f}:X\to\mu^{i}_{N}(M)
in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
with
\widehat{f}^{i}=f
.

Conversely every
g:X\to\mu^{i}_{N}(M)
in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
is of this form for a unique
f:X^{i}\to M
, namely
f=g^{i}
. This establishes a bijection which is natural in
X
and
M
.

(2)

Let
X\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
and
M\in\mathcal{C}
. If
f:M\to X^{i-N+1}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then

\cdot\cdot\cdot
X^{i-N}
X^{i-N+1}
X^{i-N+2}
\cdot\cdot\cdot
X^{i-1}
X^{i}
X^{i+1}
\cdot\cdot\cdot
\cdot\cdot\cdot
0
M

\cdot\cdot\cdot
M

\cdot\cdot\cdot
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}
d_{X}

\mathrm{Id}_{M}
\mathrm{Id}_{M}
\mathrm{Id}_{M}
\mathrm{Id}_{M}

f

d^{N-1}\circ f
0

is a morphism
\widehat{f}:\mu_{N}^{i}(M)\to X
in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
with
\widehat{f}^{i-N+1}=f
.

Conversely every
g:\mu_{N}^{i}(M)\to X
in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
is of this form for a unique morphism
f:M\to X^{i-N+1}
, namely
f=g^{i-N+1}
. This establishes a bijection which is natural in
X
and
M
.∎

Proposition 1.9
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is an additive category, then
\mu^{i}_{N}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
defines an additive functor, and all the above adjunctions become adjunctions of additive functors.

Proof.

For
f,g\in\mathcal{A}(X,Y)
it is clear by the definition of
\mu^{i}_{N}
that
\mu_{N}^{i}(f+g)=\mu_{N}^{i}(f)+\mu_{N}^{i}(g)
. Moreover the bijections from Proposition
1.8
are compatible with this addition.
∎

After having defined these standard complexes we will inspect which properties the category of
N
-complexes inherits from the ground category. We are especially intrested in limits and colimits since we need coproducts for defining our monoidal structure and colimits for the model structure.

Proposition 1.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and assume that
\mathcal{C}
admits all colimits (respectively limits). Then
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
admits all colimits (respectively limits). If
\mathcal{C}
is pointed, then
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
admits colimits and limits if
\mathcal{C}
does.

Proof.

The claim for
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
follows from the fact that
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
is the category of functors from the small category
\mathrm{I}
to the category
\mathcal{C}
by Lemma
1.2
. In particular colimits and limits in
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
are computed pointwise.

In the pointed case we need to show that the pointwise construction of limits and colimits defines an object in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
. Hence consider
X:=\mathrm{colim}_{j\in J}X_{j}
. Now
d_{X}=\mathrm{colim}_{j\in J}d_{X_{j}}
, but this implies by pointwise calculation that
d_{X}^{N}=\mathrm{colim}_{j\in J}d_{X_{j}}^{N}
and by
d_{X_{j}}^{N}=0
for all
j\in J
we get
d_{X}^{N}=0
. So
\mathrm{colim}_{j\in J}X_{j}
is again an object in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
.
An analogous argument shows the claim for limits.
∎

Proposition 1.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category, then
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
are abelian as well.

Proof.

The category
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
is abelian since it can be interpreted by Lemma
1.2
as the functor category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{I},\mathcal{A})
where kernels and cokernels are computed pointwise.
Now to show that
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is abelian it suffices to show that the kernels and cokernels in
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
of maps in the subcategory
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
are again objects in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Consider for this a morphism
\varphi:X\to Y
in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.

kernels:

Let
f:K\to X
be the kernel of
\varphi
in
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
. Then
f
is a pointwise monomorphism. Furthermore we have
f\circ d_{K}=d_{X}\circ f
, in particular one gets

f\circ d_{K}^{N}=d_{X}^{N}\circ f=0\circ f=0.

Since
f
is a pointwise monomorphism this implies
d_{K}^{N}=0
. So
K
is an object of
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
as desired.

cokernels:

Let
g:Y\to C
be the cokernel of
\varphi
in
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
, then
g
is a pointwise epimorphism and we have again that
d_{C}\circ g=g\circ d_{Y}
, which yields

\displaystyle d_{C}^{N}\circ g=g\circ d_{Y}^{N}=g\circ 0=0.

Now we get by the cancelation property that
d_{C}^{N}=0
, and so
C
is an object of
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.

All together we have that
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is abelian.
∎

2.
Interlude on combinatorics of
q
-binomial coefficients

2.1.
Background

Sign rules play a very important role in homological algebra, and hence also in the theory of chain complexes. In order to extend these to
N
-complexes for arbitrary natural numbers
N
we study
q
-binomial coefficients. These are of interest, since they arise as coefficients if we apply the Leibniz rule with
-1
replaced by a generic
q
. We also show that these vanish for primitive roots of unity, which is exactly what we desire to define a monoidal structure on
N
-complexes.

A lot of the results in this subsection are known in the general literature. However, we still show the proofs as these show the intimate relation to binomial coefficients and to supply an easy accessible reference for these results usually given as exercises.

Definition 2.1
.

In
\mathbb{Z}[q]
define:

(1)

The
q
-bracket
of
n\in\mathbb{N}
for
1<n
as

\displaystyle[n]_{q}:=1+q+q^{2}+...+q^{n-1}\text{ and }[0]_{q}:=0,[1]_{q}:=1.

(2)

The
q
-factorial
of
n\in\mathbb{N}
as

\displaystyle[n]!_{q}:=[1]_{q}[2]_{q}...[n]_{q}\text{ and }[0]!_{q}:=1.

And in
\mathbb{Q}(q)
define:

(3)

The
q
-binomial coefficient
or
Gaussian binomial coefficient
of
n
over
k
for
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
where
k\leq n
as

\displaystyle\binom{n}{k}_{q}:=\frac{[n]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}.

Remark 2.2
.

We will prove in Lemma
2.4
that actually
\binom{n}{k}_{q}
defines an element in
\mathbb{N}[q]
, i. e. a polynomial with only natural coefficients.

Example 2.3
.

Some non-trivial examples for
q
-binomial coefficients are:

\displaystyle\binom{4}{2}_{q}

\displaystyle=q^{4}+q^{3}+2q^{2}+q+1,

\displaystyle\binom{5}{2}_{q}

\displaystyle=q^{6}+q^{5}+2q^{4}+2q^{3}+2q^{2}+q+1,

\displaystyle\binom{6}{3}_{q}

\displaystyle=q^{9}+q^{8}+2q^{7}+3q^{6}+3q^{5}+3q^{4}+3q^{3}+2q^{2}+q+1.

Lemma 2.4
.

The following equations hold for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq k\leq n-1
:

(1)

\displaystyle\binom{n}{k}_{q}

\displaystyle=q^{k}\binom{n-1}{k}_{q}+\binom{n-1}{k-1}_{q},

(2)

\displaystyle\binom{n}{k}_{q}

\displaystyle=\binom{n-1}{k}_{q}+q^{n-k}\binom{n-1}{k-1}_{q}.

Proof.

(1)

We calculate explicitly:

\displaystyle q^{k}\binom{n-1}{k}_{q}+\binom{n-1}{k-1}_{q}

\displaystyle=q^{k}\frac{[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k-1]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}+\frac{[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k-1]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=q^{k}\frac{[n-1]!_{q}}{[k]_{q}[n-k-1]!_{q}[k-1]!_{q}}+\frac{[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]_{q}[n-k-1]!_{q}[k-1]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{k}[n-k]_{q}[n-1]!_{q}+[k]_{q}[n-1]!_{q}}{[k]_{q}[n-k]_{q}[n-k-1]!_{q}[k-1]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(q^{k}[n-k]_{q}+[k]_{q}\right)[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(q^{k}\sum_{i=0}^{n-k-1}q^{i}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}q^{j}\right)[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}q^{i}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}q^{j}\right)[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}q^{i}\right)[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{[n]_{q}[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}=\frac{[n]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}=\binom{n}{k}_{q}.

(2)

Again explicit calculation yields:

\displaystyle\binom{n-1}{k}_{q}+q^{n-k}\binom{n-1}{k-1}_{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k-1]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}+q^{n-k}\frac{[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k-1]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{[n-1]!_{q}}{[k]_{q}[n-k-1]!_{q}[k-1]!_{q}}+q^{n-k}\frac{[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]_{q}[n-k-1]!_{q}[k-1]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{[n-k]_{q}[n-1]!_{q}+q^{n-k}[k]_{q}[n-1]!_{q}}{[k]_{q}[n-k]_{q}[n-k-1]!_{q}[k-1]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left([n-k]_{q}+q^{n-k}[k]_{q}\right)[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-k-1}q^{i}+q^{n-k}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}q^{j}\right)[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-k-1}q^{i}+\sum_{j=n-k}^{n-1}q^{j}\right)[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}q^{i}\right)[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{[n]_{q}[n-1]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}=\frac{[n]!_{q}}{[n-k]!_{q}[k]!_{q}}=\binom{n}{k}_{q}.\qed

Remark 2.5
.

As mentioned before the above lemma in particular proves by induction
\binom{n}{k}_{q}\in\mathbb{N}[q]
, since we have
\binom{n}{0}_{q}=\binom{1}{0}_{q}=1=\binom{1}{1}_{q}=\binom{n}{n}_{q}
and
\binom{n}{1}_{q}=\binom{n}{n-1}_{q}=[n]_{q}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 2.6
.

For
R
a ring and
\xi\in R
, we denote the images of the above elements under the evaluation homomorphism

\displaystyle ev_{\xi}:\mathbb{Z}[q]

\displaystyle\to R

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\mapsto\xi

by
[n]_{\xi},[n]!_{\xi}
and
\binom{n}{k}_{\xi}
.

The following Lemma will be of use in multiple calculations and generalizes the ordinary binomial formula to a
q
-binomial formula:

Lemma 2.7
.

The following formula holds in
\mathbb{Z}[x,y,q]
:

\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(x+q^{k}y)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{q}x^{n-k}y^{k}.

Proof.

We use induction. For the induction base consider the case
n=1
, which leads

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{0}(x+q^{k}y)

\displaystyle=(x+y)=\sum_{k=0}^{1}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{q}x^{n-k}y^{k}.

Now the claim follows by the following calculation:

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{n}\left(x+q^{k}y\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\left(x+q^{k}y\right)\right)\left(x+q^{n}y\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{q}x^{n-k}y^{k}\right)\left(x+q^{n}y\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{q}x^{n-k+1}y^{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{n}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}+n}\binom{n}{k}_{q}x^{n-k}y^{k+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{q}x^{n-k+1}y^{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}q^{\frac{(k-1)(k-2)}{2}+n}\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}x^{n-k+1}y^{k}

\displaystyle=x^{n+1}+q^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}+n}y^{n+1}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{q}+q^{\frac{(k-1)(k-2)}{2}+n}\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}\right)x^{n-k+1}y^{k}

\displaystyle=x^{n+1}+q^{\frac{(n+1)n}{2}}y^{n+1}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{q}+q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}+n-k+1}\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}\right)x^{n-k+1}y^{k}

\displaystyle=x^{n+1}+q^{\frac{(n+1)n}{2}}y^{n+1}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\left(\binom{n}{k}_{q}+q^{n-k+1}\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}\right)x^{n-k+1}y^{k}

\displaystyle=x^{n+1}+q^{\frac{(n+1)n}{2}}y^{n+1}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n+1}{k}_{q}x^{n-k+1}y^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n+1}{k}_{q}x^{n-k+1}y^{k}.

Here we used in the third to last equality formula (2) from Lemma
2.4
.
∎

2.2.
Vanishing at roots of unity

Now we will show that the
q
-binomial coefficients vanish at certain primitive roots of unity. A very powerful approach for this is to use cyclic sieving. Since this needs a more involved argument and we only need a vanishing argument for
q
-binomial coefficients, we will present a less technical proof. However our proof is strongly inspired by cyclic sieving and suffices for our purposes. For the cyclic sieving argument we refer to
[
21
]
.

We first need to review some standard representation theory notions to apply them in the vanishing theorem.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
0\leq k\leq n
. Then

(1)

let the set
\left\{1,2,...,n\right\}
be denoted by
[n]
,

(2)

let
\langle(1,2,...,n)\rangle:=C_{n}\subset S_{n}
denote the
cyclic group of order
n
identified as the subgroup of the symmetric group generated by
(1,2,...,n)
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
G
be a group,
V
a finite dimensional vector space over a field
\mathsf{k}
and
\varphi:G\to\mathrm{Aut}(V)
an action of
G
on
V
. Then the
character

\chi_{\varphi}:G\to\mathsf{k}
of
\varphi
is defined by
\chi_{\varphi}(g)=\mathrm{tr}(\varphi(g))
for
g\in G
.

Remark 2.10
.

This is well defined since the trace is basis independent

Remark 2.11
.

We will denote for a ring
R
and a set
I
the free
R
-module over
I
by
\langle I\rangle_{R}
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then define the action
\varphi_{n}
of
C_{n}:=\langle(1,2,...,n)\rangle\subset S_{n}
on
\langle[n]\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
as

\varphi_{n}(1,2,...,n)(i)=(1,2,...,n)(i).

Definition 2.13
.

Let
V
be a vector space over a field
\mathsf{k}
. Then denote for
k\in\mathbb{N}
the
k
-th exterior product of
V
by
\Lambda^{k}V
.

Furthermore define for a group representation
\varphi:G\to\mathrm{Aut}({V,V})
the induced representation

\displaystyle\Lambda^{k}\varphi:G

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda^{k}V,\Lambda^{k}V)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto(\Lambda^{k}\varphi(g):\Lambda^{k}V\to\Lambda^{k}V)

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
1\leq k<n
. Then we have

0=\chi_{\Lambda^{k}\varphi_{n}}(1,2,...,n).

Proof.

Consider the basis
B:=\{e_{\{i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{k}\}}:=i_{1}\wedge i_{2}\wedge...\wedge i_{k}\mid 1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{k}\leq n\}
of
\Lambda^{k}\langle[n]\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then we get by definition

\displaystyle\Lambda^{k}\varphi_{n}(1,2,...,n)(e_{\{i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{k}\}})

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{k}\varphi_{n}(1,2,...,n)(i_{1}\wedge i_{2}\wedge...\wedge i_{k})

\displaystyle=(1,2,...,n)i_{1}\wedge(1,2,...,n)i_{2}\wedge...\wedge(1,2,...,n)i_{k}

\displaystyle=\delta e_{\{(1,2,...,n)i_{1},(1,2,...,n)i_{2},...,(1,2,...,n)i_{k}\}},

where
\delta=\pm 1
. In particular the matrix of
\Lambda^{k}\varphi_{n}(1,2,...,n)
with respect to
B
has only zeroes on the diagonal. So we get

\chi_{\Lambda^{k}\varphi_{n}}(1,2,...,n)=\mathrm{tr}\left(\Lambda^{k}\varphi_{n}(1,2,...,n)\right)=0

as claimed.∎

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
V
be an
n
-dimensional vector space over a field
\mathsf{k}
and
f:V\to V
a diagonalizable endomorphism with eigenvalues
x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n}
. Then we have for
k\in\mathbb{N}
that the endomorphism

\Lambda^{k}(f):\Lambda^{k}(V)\to\Lambda^{k}(V)

induced by
f
is diagonalizable and we have

\mathrm{tr}(\Lambda^{k}(f))=\sum_{\mathclap{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{k}\leq n}}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}...x_{i_{k}}.

Proof.

We have by assumption a basis
B:=\{b_{1},b_{2},...,b_{n}\}
of
V
consisting of eigenvectors for the respective eigenvalues of
f
. This basis induces a basis
\Lambda^{k}B
on
\Lambda^{k}(V)
. We may consider
\Lambda^{k}(f)
with respect to this basis and get

\displaystyle\Lambda^{k}(f)\left(b_{i_{1}}\wedge b_{i_{2}}\wedge...\wedge b_{i_{k}}\right)=x_{i_{1}}b_{i_{1}}\wedge x_{i_{2}}b_{i_{2}}\wedge...\wedge x_{i_{k}}b_{i_{k}}=x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}...x_{i_{k}}b_{i_{1}}\wedge b_{i_{2}}\wedge...\wedge b_{i_{k}}.

So the vectors in
\Lambda^{k}B
are eigenvectors of
\Lambda^{k}(f)
as well. Using this basis to calculate
\mathrm{tr}(\Lambda^{k}(f))
gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\Lambda^{k}(f))=\sum_{\mathclap{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{k}\leq n}}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}...x_{i_{k}}.

This finishes the proof. ∎

Definition 2.16
.

Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
0\leq k\leq n
. We denote by
e_{k,n}(x_{1},...,x_{n})
the
k
-th elementary symmetric polynomial in
n
variables
. This is defined as

\displaystyle e_{k,n}(x_{1},...,x_{n}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathclap{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{k}\leq n}}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}...x_{i_{k}}\in\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n}],

for
0<k
and for
k=0
as

\displaystyle e_{0,n}(x_{1},...,x_{n}):=

\displaystyle 1.

After this reminder on some linear algebra and representation theory the only thing missing is a connection to
q
-binomials. For this we consider the following relation between
e_{k,n}(1,...,q^{n-1})
and
\binom{n}{k}_{q}
.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k\leq n
and
1\leq n
. Then the equation

e_{k,n}(1,q,q^{2},...,q^{n-1})=q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{q}

holds in
\mathbb{Z}[q]
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
2.7
by considering
x=1
and
y=t
. More precisely we get the following sequence of identities, using for the second identity Vieta’s formula:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{q}t^{k}=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\left(1+q^{k}t\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}e_{k,n}(1,q,...,q^{n-1})t^{k}.

This gives the desired equation by comparing coefficients.
∎

Now we just need to put the collected statements together and get:

Theorem 2.18
.

Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
0<k<n
and let
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
be a primitive
n
-th root of unity. Then we have in
\mathbb{C}

\binom{n}{k}_{\xi}=0.

Proof.

First of all we know
0=\chi_{\Lambda^{k}\varphi_{n}}(1,2,...,n)
by Proposition
2.14
. Now
\varphi_{n}(1,2,...,n)
has characteristic polynomial
x^{n}-1
and so it has a basis of eigenvectors
\{v_{1},...,v_{n}\}
, where
v_{i}
has the eigenvalue
\xi^{i}
. In particular it is diagonalizable. Now by Proposition
2.15
also
\Lambda^{k}(\varphi_{n})(1,2,...,n)
is diagonalizable and we get:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\chi_{\Lambda^{k}(\varphi_{n})}(1,2,...,n)=\sum_{\mathclap{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{k}\leq n}}\xi^{i_{1}}\xi^{i_{2}}...\xi^{i_{k}}=e_{k,n}(\xi,\xi^{2},...,\xi^{n-1},\xi^{n}).

We get by
\xi^{n}=1=\xi^{0}
, Proposition
2.17
and the symmetry of the elementary symmetric polynomials that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=e_{k,n}(\xi,\xi^{2},...,\xi^{n-1},\xi^{n})=e_{k,n}(1,\xi,\xi^{2},...,\xi^{n-1})=\xi^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{\xi}.

Since
\mathbb{C}
is a field and
\xi\neq 0
we get
\binom{n}{k}_{\xi}=0
as claimed.
∎

The previous statement about
q
-binomial coefficients relies heavily on the particular structure of complex numbers.
Importantly, standard arguments, such as the cyclotomic polynomial
[
16
]
, allow to carry it over to arbitrary integral domains:

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
R
be an integral domain,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\xi\in R
a primitive
n
-th root of unity i. e. an element such that
\xi^{n}=1
and
\xi^{j}\neq 1
for all
0<j<n
. Then we have
\binom{n}{k}_{\xi}=0
for all
0<k<n
.

Proof.

We will prove this statement in two steps, firstly showing the statement for
x\in\mathbb{Z}[x]/\left(\Phi_{n}\right)
, where
\Phi_{n}
is the
n
-th cyclotomic polynomial, and in a second step that the evaluation morphism
\mathbb{Z}[x]\to R
defined by
x\mapsto\xi
factors through
\mathbb{Z}[x]/\left(\Phi_{n}\right)
.

Consider the map
\mathbb{Z}[x]/\left(\Phi_{n}\right)\to\mathbb{C}
defined by
x\mapsto\xi
for
\xi
a primitive
n
-th root of unity. This is a well defined monomorphism since
\Phi_{n}
is the
n
-th cyclotomic polynomial. We have
\binom{n}{k}_{\xi}=0
for all
0<k<n
by Theorem
2.18
since this is just the image of
\binom{n}{k}_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]/\left(\Phi_{n}\right)
we get the desired formula in

\mathbb{Z}[x]/\left(\Phi_{n}\right)
.

Now consider for an arbitrary integral domain the following composition denoting by
\mathrm{Quot}(R)
and
\overline{\mathrm{Quot}(R)}
the quotient field of
R
and its algebraic closure:

\mathbb{Z}[x]
\mathbb{Z}[x]/\left(\Phi_{n}\right)
R
\mathrm{Quot}(R)
\overline{\mathrm{Quot}(R)},
X\mapsto\xi

where we only need to show that the dashed arrow exists such that the diagram commutes. By the homomorphism theorem it suffices to check that
\Phi_{n}(\xi)=0
in
R
. This is equivalent to
\Phi_{n}(\xi)=0
in
\overline{\mathrm{Quot}(R)}
, which holds since
\Phi_{n}
is the
n
-th cyclotomic polynomial and
\xi
is an
n
-th primitive root of unity in
R
and hence also in
\overline{\mathrm{Quot}(R)}
.
∎

Remark 2.20
.

This statement heavily uses the cyclotomic polynomial
\Phi_{n}
and its properties. In particular that for every field
\mathsf{k}
with
\mathrm{char}(\mathsf{k})\nmid n
the
n
-th primitive roots of unity are roots of
\Phi_{n}
, and that
\Phi_{n}
can be defined in
\mathbb{Z}[x]
and hence is independent of the field. For the precise arguments that show this we refer to Lang’s book
[
16
]
.

3.
Lifting functors

In this section we will apply the tools developed in the previous sections to define functors on
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
using functors on
\mathcal{C}
. This is of particular interest since a lot of structures on a category can be encoded using functors. First we will consider ordinary functors, but later we will discuss how to lift bifunctors which will be essential for constructing the monoidal structure on the category of
N
-complexes.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be categories and
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
a functor. Then define for an
\infty
-complex
X
in
\mathcal{C}
the
\infty
-complex
F(X)
in
\mathcal{D}
as

\displaystyle F(X)^{i}

\displaystyle:=F(X^{i})

\displaystyle(d_{F(X)}:F(X^{i})\to F(X^{i+1}))

\displaystyle:=F(d_{X}:X^{i}\to X^{i+1}).

Furthermore define for a morphism of
\infty
-complexes
f:X\to Y
, the family of morphisms
(F(f)^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
as

\displaystyle F(f)^{i}=F(f^{i}).

Proposition 3.2
.

The above assignments define a functor
F:\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{D})
.

Proof.

By construction
F(X)
is an
\infty
-complex in
\mathcal{D}
, so it just remains to be checked that for a morphism of
\infty
-complexes
f:X\to Y
, the family
F(f)
is indeed a morphism of
\infty
-complexes, since functoriality follows by levelwise functoriality. Hence we need to show that the diagram

F(X^{i})=F(X)^{i}
F(X^{i+1})=F(X)^{i+1}
F(Y^{i+1})=F(Y)^{i+1}
F(Y^{i})=F(Y)^{i}
d_{F(X)}=F(d_{X})
d_{F(Y)}=F(d_{Y})
F(f)^{i}=F(f^{i})
F(f)^{i+1}=F(f^{i+1})

commutes for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a functor, it suffices to show that

X^{i}
X^{i+1}
Y^{i+1}
Y^{i}
d_{X}
d_{Y}
f^{i}
f^{i+1}

commutes for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, but this holds since
f
is a morphism of
\infty
-complexes in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be pointed categories,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
a pointed functor, i. e.
F(0)\cong 0
for
0
a zero object in
\mathcal{C}
. Then the functor
F:\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{D})
induces a functor
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{D})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.2
it suffices to check that
F(X)
is an
N
-complex in
\mathcal{D}
if
X
is an
N
-complex in
\mathcal{C}
, but this is clear, since

d_{F(X)}^{N}=F(d_{X}^{N})=F(0)=0,

and so
F(X)
is an
N
-complex.∎

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{D}
be categories and
F:\mathcal{C}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{D}:G
a pair of adjoint functors. Then
F:\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{D}):G
is also a pair of adjoint functors.

Proof.

Since
F,G
are adjoint functors we have natural transformations
\eta:\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\to G\circ F
and
\epsilon:F\circ G\to\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{D}}
such that the following diagrams commute:

F
FGF,
F
G
GFG
G.
F\eta
\epsilon F
\eta G
G\epsilon
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}

Now since
\eta
and
\epsilon
are natural transformations, we get that the following squares commute for all
X,Y\in\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
:

X^{j}
G\circ F(X^{j})
G\circ F(X^{j+1})
X^{j+1}
F\circ G(Y^{j+1})
F\circ G(Y^{j})
Y^{j}
Y^{j+1}.
\eta_{X^{j}}
\eta_{X^{j+1}}
d_{X}
G\circ F(d_{X})
\epsilon_{Y^{j+1}}
\epsilon_{Y^{j}}
F\circ G(d_{Y})
d_{Y}

Hence
(\eta_{X^{j}}:X^{j}\to G\circ F(X^{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
(\eta_{Y^{j}}:F\circ G(Y^{j})\to Y^{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
define morphisms of
\infty
-
complexes. These define by construction natural transformations
\eta_{\mathrm{C}_{\infty}}:\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})}\to G\circ F
and
\epsilon_{\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})}:F\circ G\to\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{D})}
. Furthermore the diagrams

F
FGF
F
G
GFG
G
F\eta_{\mathrm{C}_{\infty}}
\epsilon_{\mathrm{C}_{\infty}}F
\eta_{\mathrm{C}_{\infty}}G
G\epsilon_{\mathrm{C}_{\infty}}
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}

commute levelwise by construction and so they commute in general. This means that
\eta_{\mathrm{C}_{\infty}}
and
\epsilon_{\mathrm{C}_{\infty}}
define a counit and unit for
F
and
G
such that the triangle identities hold, which proves the claim.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be pointed categories,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
F:\mathcal{C}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{D}:G
an adjunction of functors. Then
F:\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{D}):G
is also a pair of adjoint functors.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.4
we get an adjunction
F:\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{D}):G
. Now by Corollary
3.3
it suffices to show that
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
and
G:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
are actually pointed functors. Consider the following isomorphisms induced by the adjunction for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
Y\in\mathcal{D}
:

\displaystyle\{*\}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}(0,F(Y))\cong\mathcal{D}(G(0),Y)

\displaystyle\{*\}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{D}(X,F(0))\cong\mathcal{D}(G(X),0).

So
G(0)
is an initial object and
F(0)
is a final object. Now since
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are pointed we get
G(0)\cong 0
and
F(0)\cong 0
which finishes the claim.
∎

The following definition is a general construction which can be used to encode the property of bifunctors respecting an enriched structure in the sense that they commute with the operations coming from this structure.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{D},\otimes_{\mathcal{D}},\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{D}},\alpha_{\mathcal{D}},\lambda_{\mathcal{D}},\rho_{\mathcal{D}})
be a symmetric monoidal category and
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be categories enriched over it.
We define
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
as the category consisting of objects of the form
(X,Y)
, where
X\in\mathcal{C},Y\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, and morphism objects

\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\left((X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2})\right):=\mathcal{C}(X_{1},X_{2})\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(Y_{1},Y_{2}).

Now we will need some kind of lifting for bifunctors of the form
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
since we want to construct a monoidal structure on the category of
N
-complexes. Similar to the ordinary case of chain complexes we need some kind of twisted Leibniz rule. We say twisted, because the ordinary Leibniz rule just works for
2
-complexes or
N
-complexes over rings with characteristic
2
. This is owing to the fact that
-1
is a primitive second root of unity, which will be clear by the proof of Proposition
3.11
. Hence we will define our lifted functors using a Leibniz rule which involves arbitrary elements of a ring. Furthermore we will show that if these elements are suitable roots of unity, we indeed get functors of
N
-complexes and not just
\infty
-complexes.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}
be categories enriched over
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
such that
\mathcal{D}
admits countable coproducts,
\xi\in R
, and let

F:\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}

be an additive
R
-linear functor. Then define for objects
X\in\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
and
Y\in\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
the
\infty
-complex
F_{\xi}(X,Y)
in
\mathcal{D}
by

F_{\xi}(X,Y)^{n}:=\bigoplus_{i+j=n}F(X^{i},Y^{j})

with the differential defined on summands by

d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}\circ\iota_{i,j}:=\iota_{i+1,j}\circ F(d_{X},\mathrm{Id})+\xi^{i}\iota_{i,j+1}\circ F(\mathrm{Id},d_{Y}).

Here
\iota_{k,l}
denotes the canonical inclusion

F(X^{k},Y^{l})\to\bigoplus_{i+j=n}F(X^{i},Y^{j})

for
k+l=n
.

Furthermore for morphisms of
\infty
-complexes
f:X\to X^{\prime}
and
g:Y\to Y^{\prime}
define the family
\left(F_{\xi}(f,g)^{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
by

F_{\xi}(f,g)^{n}:=\bigoplus_{i+j=n}F(f^{i},g^{j}).

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}
be categories enriched over
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
such that
\mathcal{D}
admits countable coproducts,
\xi\in R
, and let

F:\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}

be an additive
R
-linear functor. Then the assignments from Definition
3.7
define a functor

F_{\xi}(\_,\_):\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\to\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{D}).

Proof.

It suffices to check for
f:X\to Y
and
g:X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
that

F_{\xi}(f,g)\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,X^{\prime})}=d_{F_{\xi}(Y,Y^{\prime})}\circ F_{\xi}(f,g).

Since by definition
F_{\xi}(\_,\_)
is functorial as well as it has the right codomain. Hence we check the statement on direct summands of the form
F(X^{i},X^{\prime j})
:

\displaystyle F_{\xi}(f,g)^{n}\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,X^{\prime})}\circ\iota_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}=n}F(f^{i^{\prime}},g^{j^{\prime}})\circ\left(\iota_{i+1,j}\circ F(d_{X},\mathrm{Id})+\xi^{i}\iota_{i,j+1}\circ F(\mathrm{Id},d_{X^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle=\iota_{i+1,j}\circ F(f^{i+1}\circ d_{X},g^{j})+\xi^{i}\iota_{i,j+1}\circ F(f^{i},g^{j+1}\circ d_{X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\iota_{i+1,j}\circ F(d_{Y}\circ f^{i},g^{j})+\xi^{i}\iota_{i,j+1}\circ F(f^{i},d_{Y^{\prime}}\circ g^{j})

\displaystyle=\left(\iota_{i+1,j}\circ F(d_{Y},\mathrm{Id})+\xi^{i}\iota_{i,j+1}\circ F(\mathrm{Id},d_{Y^{\prime}})\right)\circ F(f^{i},g^{j})

\displaystyle=d_{F_{\xi}(X,X^{\prime})}\circ\iota_{i,j}\circ F(f^{i},g^{j})

\displaystyle=d_{F_{\xi}(Y,Y^{\prime})}\circ\left(\bigoplus_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}=n}F(f^{i^{\prime}},g^{j^{\prime}})\right)\circ\iota_{i,j}

\displaystyle=d_{F_{\xi}(Y,Y^{\prime})}\circ F_{\xi}(f,g)\circ\iota_{i,j}.\qed

Having this general definition we can use our work on
q
-binomial coefficients to prove that
F_{\xi}(\_,\_)
indeed defines a bifunctor on the category of
N
-complexes for
\xi
a suitable root of unity. To make precise what suitable means we define the following:

Definition 3.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},N,\xi)
be a triple consisting of an additive category
\mathcal{C}
enriched over
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
for
R
a ring,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\xi\in R
. Then we say that
(\mathcal{C},N,\xi)

allows the twisted Leibniz rule
if one of the following holds:

(i)

Every morphism in
\mathcal{C}
is
p
-torsion for
p
a prime number i. e.
pf=0
for all morphisms
f
,
\xi=1
and
N=p^{n}
for some natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)

The element
\xi\in R
is a primitve
N
-th root of unity.

(iii)

Every morphism in
\mathcal{C}
is
p
-torsion for
p
a prime number, the element
\xi\in R
is a primitive
k
-th root of unity and
N=p^{n}k
for some natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 3.10
.

In the above definition, case (iii) specializes to case (i) by setting
\xi=1
and
k=1
.
Furthermore one can think of case (iii) as an enhancement of case (ii), by applying the structure of case (i) to case (ii).

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
R
be an integral domain,
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}
be additive categories enriched over
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
such that
\mathcal{D}
admits countable coproducts,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\xi\in R
and let
F:\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
be an additive bifunctor. Then the functor defined in Definition
3.7
restricts to a functor

F_{\xi}(\_,\_):\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})

if
(\mathcal{D},N,\xi)
allows the twisted Leibniz rule.

Proof.

Since we already know by Proposition
3.8
that
F_{\xi}(\_,\_):\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\to\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{D})
is a functor we only need to show in all cases that for
N
-complexes
X,Y
we get that
F_{\xi}(X,Y)
is an
N
-complex as well. For this we consider the three cases in Definition
3.9
separately and calculate omitting inclusions:

(i)

Since every morphism in
\mathcal{D}
is a
p
-torsion for a prime number
p
and
N=p^{n}
, we may use that for all morphisms
f
in
\mathcal{D}
we have
pf=f+f+...+f=0\in\mathcal{D}
, hence it suffices to consider
\mathcal{D}
as enriched over
R/pR
. Now we may use that
\binom{p}{k}=0
in
R/pR
for all
0<k<p
. First we generalize this to
\binom{N}{k}=0
if
0<k<N
.
For this consider the following calculation in
R/pR[x,y]

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\dbinom{N}{k}x^{N-k}y^{k}

\displaystyle=(x+y)^{N}=(((x+y)^{p})^{p}...)^{p}=(((x^{p}+y^{p})^{p})^{p}...)^{p}

\displaystyle=x^{p^{n}}+y^{p^{n}}=x^{N}+y^{N}.

So the claim holds by comparing coefficients. Now using this we get

\displaystyle d_{F_{1}(X,Y)}^{N}=

\displaystyle d^{N-1}(F(d_{X},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{N-2}(F(d_{X}^{2},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+F(d_{X},d_{Y})+F(d_{X},d_{Y})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d^{2}_{Y}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{N-2}(F(d_{X}^{2},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+2F(d_{X},d_{Y})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d^{2}_{Y}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{N-3}(F(d_{X}^{3},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+3F(d_{X}^{2},d_{Y})+3F(d_{X},d_{Y}^{2})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d^{3}_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N}\dbinom{N}{k}F(d^{N-k}_{X},d^{k}_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(d_{X}^{N},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}^{N})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

and so
F_{\xi}(X,Y)
is an
N
-complex. Here we used in the second to last equality
\binom{N}{k}=0
if
0<k<N
.

(ii)

In this case we will use the two formulas from Proposition
2.4
and Corollary
2.19
.
Here we need for
\binom{N}{k}_{\xi}=0
that
\xi
is a primitive
N
-th root of unity.
Analogously to Lemma
2.7
we calculate for each summand (cf. Remark
3.12
):

\displaystyle\left.d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N}\right|_{F(X^{i},Y^{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{N-1}\left(F(d_{X},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+\xi^{i}F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y})\middle)\right|_{F(X^{i},Y^{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{N-2}\left(F(d_{X}^{2},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+\xi^{i+1}F(d_{X},d_{Y})+\xi^{i}F(d_{X},d_{Y})+\xi^{2i}F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d^{2}_{Y})\middle)\right|_{F(X^{i},Y^{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{N-2}\left(\binom{2}{2}_{\xi}F(d_{X}^{2},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+\xi^{i}\binom{2}{1}_{\xi}F(d_{X},d_{Y})+\xi^{2i}\binom{2}{0}_{\xi}F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d^{2}_{Y})\middle)\right|_{X^{i}\otimes Y^{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{N-3}\left(\binom{2}{2}_{\xi}F(d_{X}^{3},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+\xi^{i+2}\binom{2}{2}_{\xi}F(d_{X}^{2},d_{Y})+\xi^{i}\binom{2}{1}_{\xi}F(d_{X}^{2},d_{Y})\right.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left.\xi^{2i+1}\binom{2}{1}_{\xi}F(d_{X},d_{Y}^{2})+\xi^{2i}\binom{2}{0}_{\xi}F(d_{X}d^{2}_{Y})+\xi^{3i}\binom{2}{0}_{\xi}F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d^{3}_{Y})\middle)\right|_{F(X^{i},Y^{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{N-3}\left(\binom{3}{3}_{\xi}F(d_{X}^{3},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+\xi^{i}\binom{3}{2}_{\xi}F(d_{X}^{2},d_{Y})+\xi^{2i}\binom{3}{1}_{\xi}F(d_{X},d_{Y}^{2})+\xi^{3i}\binom{3}{0}_{\xi}F(d_{X},d^{3}_{Y})\middle)\right|_{F(X^{i},Y^{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\sum_{k=0}^{N}\xi^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{N}{N-k}_{\xi}F(d_{X}^{N-k},d_{Y}^{k})\right|_{F(X^{i},Y^{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(d_{X}^{N},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}^{N})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Hence

\displaystyle d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N}=

\displaystyle F(d_{X}^{N},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}^{N})=0

on all of
X\otimes_{\xi}Y
and so it is an
N
-complex as desired.

(iii)

Since this case is a combination of the above cases we will use both of the above calculations. First we will use that for
\xi\in R
a primitive
k
-th root of unity and
l\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{k}=

\displaystyle F(d_{X}^{k},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}^{k}).

Using this we observe that

\displaystyle d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N}

\displaystyle=d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{p^{n}k}=d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{k}\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{k}\circ...\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{k},

with
p^{n}
factors in the factorization. Now consider
d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{k}
instead of
d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}
. The morphism
d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{k}
now behaves the same way as
d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}
, with
\xi
replaced by
1
and
N
replaced by
p^{n}
, yielding an analogous calculation to case (1):

\displaystyle d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N}=

\displaystyle d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-k}(F(d_{X}^{k},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}^{k}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-2k}(F(d_{X}^{2k},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+2F(d_{X}^{k},d_{Y}^{k})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}^{2k}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{\xi}(d_{X,Y}^{N-3k}(F(d_{X}^{3k},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+3F(d_{X}^{2k},d_{Y}^{k})+3F(d_{X}^{k}d_{Y}^{2k})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}^{3k}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{p^{n}}\dbinom{p^{n}}{l}F(d^{(p^{n}-l)k}_{X},d^{lk}_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(d_{X}^{p^{n}k},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}^{p^{n}k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(d_{X}^{N},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}^{N})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

so
F_{\xi}(X,Y)
is again an
N
-complex.

∎

Remark 3.12
.

The above proof can be also seen as an application of the formula

\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}(x+q^{k}y)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{k}_{q}x^{N-k}y^{k}

from Lemma
2.7
by setting
x:=F(d_{X},\mathrm{Id}_{Y}),y:=F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y})
and
q:=\xi
. Observing that
\xi
is a
k
-th primitive root of unity with
k\mid N
yields that every distinct power of
\xi
appears precisely
\frac{N}{k}
times and so we get the desired result. However, we proved this
q
-binomial formula in the ring
\mathbb{Z}[x,y,q]
, so we would need a general argument to apply it here, which we do not think is necessary, since we only applied it in full strength in case (2).

Another important type of functors we need to lift are functors which are covariant in one variable and contravariant in the other since these arise as internal homs. In particular they are of interest if we want to construct a closed monoidal structure on the category of
N
-complexes.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}
be categories enriched over
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
such that
\mathcal{D}
admits countable products,
\xi\in R
and
F(\_,\_):\mathcal{C}^{op}\otimes_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
an
R
-linear bifunctor. Then define for two
\infty
-complexes
X,Y
the
\infty
-complex
F_{\xi}(X,Y)
as

\displaystyle F_{\xi}(X,Y)^{i}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}F(X^{j},Y^{i+j})

with the differential defined on factors by

\displaystyle\pi_{j,i+j}\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}:=F(X^{j},d_{Y})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i-1}F(d_{X},Y^{i+j})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}.

Here
\pi_{k,l}
denotes the canonical morphism

\pi_{k,l}:\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}F(X^{j},Y^{i+j})\to F(X^{k},Y^{l})

for
i=l-k
.

Furthermore define for a morphism of
\infty
-complexes
f:X\to X^{\prime}
the family of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
:

\displaystyle F_{\xi}(f,Y)

\displaystyle:=\left(\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}F(f^{j},Y^{i+j}):F_{\xi}(X^{\prime},Y)^{i}\to F_{\xi}(X,Y)^{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},

and for
f:Y\to Y^{\prime}
the family of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle F_{\xi}(X,f)^{i}

\displaystyle:=\left(\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}F(X^{j},f^{i+j}):F_{\xi}(X,Y)^{i}\to F_{\xi}(X,Y^{\prime})^{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}
be additive categories enriched over
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
such that
\mathcal{D}
admits countable products,
\xi\in R
and
F(\_,\_):\mathcal{C}^{op}\otimes_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
an
R
-linear bifunctor. Then
the assignments from Definition
3.13
define a functor

\displaystyle F_{\xi}(\_,\_):\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})^{op}\times\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\to\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{D}).

Proof.

One just needs to check that for
f:X\to X^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
and
Y\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, respectively
Y\in\mathcal{C}
and
f:X\to X^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, the families
F_{\xi}(f,Y)
and
F_{\xi}(Y,f)
indeed define morphisms of
\infty
-complexes since the assignments are levelwise products of functors. Consider first

F_{\xi}(f,Y):F_{\xi}(X^{\prime},Y)\to F_{\xi}(X,Y)

for
f:X\to X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(X,X^{\prime})
and
Y\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. So we need to show that

F_{\xi}(X^{\prime},Y)^{i-1}
F_{\xi}(X,Y)^{i-1}
F_{\xi}(X^{\prime},Y)^{i}
F_{\xi}(X,Y)^{i}
d_{F_{\xi}(X^{\prime},Y)}
d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}
F_{\xi}(f,Y)^{i-1}
F_{\xi}(f,Y)^{i}

commutes. This we check on factors:

\displaystyle\pi_{j,i+j}\circ\left(F_{\xi}(f,Y)^{i}\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X^{\prime},Y)}\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{j,i+j}\circ\prod_{j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}F(f^{j^{\prime}},Y^{i+j^{\prime}})\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X^{\prime},Y)}

\displaystyle=F(f^{j},Y)\circ\pi_{j,i+j}\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X^{\prime},Y)}

\displaystyle=F(f^{j},Y)\circ(F({X^{\prime}}^{j},d_{Y})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(d_{X^{\prime}},Y^{i+j})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j})

\displaystyle=F(f^{j},d_{Y})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(f^{j}\circ d_{X^{\prime}},Y^{j+i})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}

\displaystyle=F(f^{j},d_{Y})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F((d_{X^{\prime}}\circ f^{j})^{op},Y^{j+i})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}

\displaystyle=F(f^{j},d_{Y})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F((f^{j+1}\circ d_{X})^{op},Y^{j+i})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}

\displaystyle=F(f^{j},d_{Y})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(d_{X}\circ f^{j+1},Y^{j+i})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}

\displaystyle=F(X,d_{Y})\circ F(f^{j},Y^{i+j-1})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(d_{X},Y^{j+i})\circ F(f^{j+1},Y^{j+i})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}

\displaystyle=\left(F(X,d_{Y})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(d_{X},Y^{j+i})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}\right)\circ\prod_{j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}F(f^{j^{\prime}},Y^{i+j^{\prime}-1})

\displaystyle=\pi_{j,i+j}\circ(d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}\circ F_{\xi}(f,Y)^{i-1}).

Now we show for
F_{\xi}(Y,f):F_{\xi}(Y,X)\to F_{\xi}(Y,X^{\prime})
for
Y\in\mathcal{C}
and
f:X\to X^{\prime}
a morphism in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
that the diagram

F_{\xi}(Y,X)^{i-1}
F_{\xi}(Y,X^{\prime})^{i-1}
F_{\xi}(Y^{\prime},X)^{i}
F_{\xi}(Y,X^{\prime})^{i}
d_{F_{\xi}(Y,X)}
d_{F_{\xi}(Y,X^{\prime})}
F_{\xi}(Y,f)^{i-1}
F_{\xi}(Y,f)^{i}

indeed commutes. Again we check on factors:

\displaystyle\pi_{j,i+j}\circ\left(F_{\xi}(Y,f)^{i}\circ d_{F_{\xi}(Y,X)}\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{j,i+j}\circ\left(\prod_{j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}F(Y^{j^{\prime}},f^{i+j^{\prime}})\right)\circ d_{F_{\xi}(Y,X)}

\displaystyle=F(Y^{j},f^{i+j})\circ\pi_{j,i+j}\circ d_{F_{\xi}(Y,X)}

\displaystyle=F(Y^{j},f^{i+j})\circ\left(F(Y^{j},d_{X})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(d_{Y},X^{i+j})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}\right)

\displaystyle=F(Y^{j},f^{i+j})\circ F(Y^{j},d_{X})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(Y^{j},f^{i+j})\circ F(d_{Y},X^{i+j})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}

\displaystyle=F(Y^{j},f^{i+j}\circ d_{X})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(d_{Y},f^{i+j})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}

\displaystyle=F(Y^{j},d_{X}\circ f^{i+j-1})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(d_{Y},X^{i+j})\circ F(Y^{j+1},f^{i+j})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}

\displaystyle=F(Y^{j},d_{X})\circ F(Y^{j},f^{i+j-1})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(d_{Y},X^{i+j})\circ F(Y^{j+1},f^{i+j})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}

\displaystyle=\left(F(Y^{j},d_{X})\circ\pi_{j,i+j-1}-\xi^{i}F(d_{Y},X^{i+j})\circ\pi_{j+1,i+j}\right)\circ\left(\prod_{j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}F(Y^{j^{\prime}},f^{i+j^{\prime}-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{j,i+j}\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}\circ F_{\xi}(Y,f)^{i-1}.

Here the shift in
Y
between the fourth and sixth line occurs since
F(\_,X)
is contravariant.
∎

Similarly to the case of additive bifunctors, we want that
F_{\xi}(\_,\_)
again defines a functor on the category of
N
-complexes for certain
\xi
and
F(\_,\_)
a contravariant/covariant bifunctor.

Theorem 3.15
.

Let
R
be an integral domain,
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}
be additive categories enriched over
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
,
\xi\in R
such that
\mathcal{D}
admits countable products,
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(\mathcal{D},N,\xi)
allows the twisted Leibniz rule and let
F(\_,\_):\mathcal{C}^{op}\otimes_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
be an
R
-linear bifunctor. Then the functor

\displaystyle F_{\xi}(\_,\_):\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})^{op}\times\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{D})

induces a functor

\displaystyle F_{\xi}(\_,\_):\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})^{op}\times\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{D}).

Proof.

By Proposition
3.14
we have that
F_{\xi}(\_,\_)
induces a functor
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})^{op}\times\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\to\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{D})
, so it suffices to check in all cases, that for
X\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
and
Y\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
the complex
F_{\xi}(X,Y)
is again an
N
-complex. This is analogous to the proof of Theorem
4.10
, still we give for completeness reasons the proof. Similarly to Theorem
4.10
we will check all three cases in Definition
3.9
separately. Furthermore we omit for legibility the projections in the calculations.

(i)

Since every morphism in
\mathcal{D}
is a
p
-torsion for a prime number
p
and
N=p^{n}
, we may use that for all morphisms
f
in
\mathcal{D}
we have
pf=0\in\mathcal{D}
, hence it suffices to consider
\mathcal{D}
as enriched over
R/pR
. Now we may use in
R/pR
that
\binom{N}{k}=0
for all
0<k<N
just like in Theorem
3.11
.
Using this one gets

\displaystyle\pi_{j,i+j}\circ d_{F_{1}(X,Y)}^{N}=

\displaystyle(F(d_{X},Y^{i+j})-F(X^{j},d_{Y}))\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(F(d^{2}_{X},Y^{i+j})-F(d_{X},d_{Y})-F(d_{X},d_{Y})+F(X^{j},d^{2}_{Y}))\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(F(d^{2}_{X},Y^{i+j})-2F(d_{X},d_{Y})+F(X^{j},d^{2}_{Y}))\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(F(d^{3}_{X},Y^{i+j})-3F(d^{2}_{X},d_{Y})+3F(d_{X},d^{2}_{Y})-F(X^{j},d^{3}_{Y}))\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}(-1)^{k}\dbinom{N}{k}F(d^{N-k}_{X},d^{k}_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(d^{N}_{X},Y^{i+j})+(-1)^{N}F(X^{j},d^{N}_{Y}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

and so
F_{\xi}(X,Y)
is an
N
-complex.

(ii)

In this case we will use the following two formulas from Proposition
2.4
and Corollary
2.19
:
Where we need for
\binom{N}{k}_{\xi}=0
that
\xi
is an
N
-th root of unity.
Now we calculate analogously to the formula of Lemma
2.7

d^{N}_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}
for each summand, keeping track of the respective degrees and setting
x=F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}),\;y=-F(d_{X},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})
and
q=\xi
:

\displaystyle\pi_{j,i+j}\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(F(X^{j},d_{Y})-\xi^{i}F(d_{X},Y^{i+j})\right)\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(F(X^{j},d^{2}_{Y})-\xi^{i}F(d_{X},d_{Y})-\xi^{i+1}F(d_{X},d_{Y})+\xi^{2i}F(d^{2}_{X},Y^{i+j})\right)\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\binom{2}{0}_{\xi}F(X^{j},d^{2}_{Y})-\xi^{i}\binom{2}{1}_{\xi}F(d_{X},d_{Y})+\xi^{2i}\binom{2}{2}_{\xi}F(d^{2}_{X},Y^{i+j})\right)\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\binom{2}{0}_{\xi}F(X^{j},d^{3}_{Y})-\xi^{i+2}\binom{2}{0}_{\xi}F(d_{X},d^{2}_{Y})-\xi^{i}\binom{2}{1}_{\xi}F(d_{X},d^{2}_{Y})\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\xi^{2i+1}\binom{2}{1}_{\xi}F(d^{2}_{X},d_{Y})+\xi^{2i}\binom{2}{2}_{\xi}F(d^{2}_{X},d_{Y})-\xi^{3i}\binom{2}{2}_{\xi}F(d^{3}_{X},Y^{i+j})\right)\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\binom{3}{0}_{\xi}F(X^{j},d^{3}_{Y})-\xi^{i}\binom{3}{1}_{\xi}F(d_{X},d^{2}_{Y})+\xi^{2i}\binom{3}{2}_{\xi}F(d^{2}_{X},d_{Y})-\xi^{3i}\binom{3}{3}_{\xi}F(d^{3}_{X},Y^{i+j})\right)\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N}\xi^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\binom{N}{N-k}_{\xi}(-1)^{k}F(d^{k}_{X},d^{N-k}_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(d^{N}_{X},Y^{i+j})+(-1)^{k}F(X^{j},d^{N}_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Hence

d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N}=F(d^{N}_{X},Y^{i+j})+F(X^{j},d^{N}_{Y})=0

on all factors of
F_{\xi}(X,Y)
and so it is an
N
-complex as desired.

(iii)

Since this case is a combination of the above two we will use both of the above calculations. First we will use that for
\xi\in R
a primitive
k
-th root of unity and
l\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{k}=

\displaystyle F(d_{X}^{k},\mathrm{Id}_{Y})+(-1)^{k}F(\mathrm{Id}_{X},d_{Y}^{k}).

Using this we observe that the following holds:

\displaystyle d_{X\otimes_{\xi}Y}^{N}

\displaystyle=d_{X\otimes_{\xi}Y}^{p^{n}k}=d_{X\otimes_{\xi}Y}^{k}\circ d_{X\otimes_{\xi}Y}^{k}\circ...\circ d_{X\otimes_{\xi}Y}^{k},

with
p^{n}
factors in the composition. Now consider
d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{k}
instead of
d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}
. The morphism
d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{k}
now behaves the same way as
d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}
, with
\xi
replaced by
1
and
N
replaced by
p^{n}
, yielding an analogous calculation to case (1):

\displaystyle\pi_{j,i+j}\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N}=

\displaystyle\left(F(d^{k}_{X},Y^{i+j})+(-1)^{k}F(X^{j},d^{k}_{Y})\right)\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(F(d^{2k}_{X},Y^{i+j})+(-1)^{k}2F(d^{k}_{X},d^{k}_{Y})+(-1)^{2k}F(X^{j},d^{2k}_{Y})\right)\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-2k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left((F(d^{3k}_{X},Y^{i+j})+(-1)^{k}3F(d^{2k}_{X},d^{k}_{Y})+(-1)^{2k}3F(d^{k}_{X},d^{2k}_{Y})+(-1)^{3k}F(X^{j},d^{3k}_{Y})\right)\circ d_{F_{\xi}(X,Y)}^{N-3k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{p^{n}}\dbinom{p^{n}}{l}(-1)^{lk}F(d^{(p^{n}-l)k}_{X},d^{lk}_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(d^{p^{n}k}_{X},Y^{i+j})+(-1)^{nk}F(X^{j},d^{p^{n}k}_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(d^{N}_{X},Y^{i+j})+(-1)^{nk}F(X^{j},d^{N}_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

So
F_{\xi}(X,Y)
is again an
N
-complex.
∎

Having established these tools for the lifting of functors, we can finally construct the monoidal structure on the category of
N
-complexes and show that it is closed if the underlying category is the category of modules over an integral domain.

4.
Monoidal structure

In this section we will generalize the tensor product on the category of chain complexes to arbitrary
N
-complexes. Since we want to do this in a very general context we need to introduce first the notions of countable distributive monoidal categories and monoidal categories enriched over a ring
R
. This will allow us to use liftings of bifunctors from the previous section to define a monoidal structure.

4.1.
Background on distributive monoidal structures

First we will discuss general distributivity properties for monoidal categories in order to use the liftings from the previous section.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbb{I},\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
be a monoidal category that admits finite coproducts. Then define the following morphisms:

(1)

For
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}
the
left distributivity morphism

\displaystyle{}_{X,Y,Z}\delta:(X\otimes Y)\oplus(X\otimes Z)\to X\otimes(Y\oplus Z),

is the morphism induced by the universal property of the coproduct in the following diagram:

X\otimes Y
X\otimes Z
(X\otimes Y)\oplus(X\otimes Z)
X\otimes(Y\oplus Z)
.
\iota_{X\otimes Y}
\iota_{X\otimes Z}
\mathrm{Id}_{X}\otimes\iota_{Y}
\mathrm{Id}_{X}\otimes\iota_{Z}

(2)

For
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}
the
right distributivity morphism

\displaystyle\delta_{Y,Z,X}:(Y\otimes X)\oplus(Z\otimes X)\to(Y\oplus Z)\otimes X,

is the morphism induced by the universal property of the coproduct and the morphisms

\displaystyle\iota_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{X}

\displaystyle:(Y\otimes X)\to(Y\oplus Z)\otimes X

\displaystyle\iota_{Z}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{X}

\displaystyle:(Z\otimes X)\to(Y\oplus Z)\otimes X.

Remark 4.2
.

By construction the left distributivity morphism defines a natural transformations between the two functors

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle(X,Y,Z)

\displaystyle\mapsto(X\otimes Y)\oplus(X\otimes Z)

\displaystyle(X,Y,Z)

\displaystyle\mapsto X\otimes(Y\oplus Z).

The same holds for the right distributivity morphism and

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle(X,Y,Z)

\displaystyle\mapsto(Y\otimes X)\oplus(Z\otimes X)

\displaystyle(X,Y,Z)

\displaystyle\mapsto(Y\oplus Z)\otimes X.

Definition 4.3
.

A monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbb{I},\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
, is called

(1)

distributive
if it is additive, the morphisms
{}_{X,Y,Z}\delta
and
\delta_{Y,Z,X}
are isomorphisms for all
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}
and
X\otimes 0\cong 0\cong 0\otimes X
for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
.

(2)

countable distributive
if
\mathcal{C}
is distributive, admits countable coproducts and for all
X,Y_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
, for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
the canonical morphisms

\displaystyle\tau_{X,Y_{i}}

\displaystyle:\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(X\otimes Y_{i})\to X\otimes\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}Y_{i}

\displaystyle\sigma_{Y_{i},X}

\displaystyle:\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(Y_{i}\otimes X)\to\left(\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}Y_{i}\right)\otimes X

are isomorphisms.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes_{\mathcal{C}},\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}},\lambda_{\mathcal{C}},\rho_{\mathcal{C}})
be a monoidal category enriched over a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{D},\otimes_{\mathcal{D}},\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{D}},\alpha_{\mathcal{D}},\lambda_{\mathcal{D}},\rho_{\mathcal{D}})
.
We call
\mathcal{C}
a
\mathcal{D}
-monoidal
category if the bifunctor
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
and structure morphisms factor through the canonical functor
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{C}
, which is the identity on objects and
\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}
on morphism spaces.

Remark 4.5
.

Definition
4.4
implies that
\_\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}Y
,
X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\_
and the natural transformations involved in the monoidal structure of
\mathcal{C}
are in fact
\mathcal{D}
-enriched functors and natural transformations of
\mathcal{D}
-enriched functors.

Now we have a good setup for applying the constructions from Section 3 and we can define the monoidal structure on
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
. However it is not yet clear that this indeed defines a monoidal structure since we are in a very general setup at the moment.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbb{I},\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
be a countable distributive
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
-monoidal category, for
R
a commutative ring, and
\xi\in R
. Then define the following on
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
:

(1)

A functor
\_\otimes_{\xi}\_:\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
just as in Definition
3.7
using the bifunctor
\_\otimes\_:\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
.

(2)

The
\infty
-complex
\mathbb{I}_{\xi}:=\mu^{0}_{1}(\mathbb{I})
.

(3)

The morphism
\alpha_{\xi,X,Y,Z}:(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\to X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)
levelwise defined as (cf. Remark
4.7
)

(\alpha_{\xi,X,Y,Z})^{j}:=\left(\bigoplus_{l+m=n}\sigma_{X^{i}\otimes Y^{k},Z^{l}}\right)\circ\gamma_{l,i,k}^{-1}\circ\left(\bigoplus_{i+k+l=n}\alpha_{X^{i},Y^{k},Z^{l}}\right)\circ\gamma_{i,k,l}\circ\left(\bigoplus_{i+j=n}\tau^{-1}_{X^{i},Y^{k}\otimes Z^{l}}\right).

Here
\gamma_{i,k,l}
is the canonical natural isomorphism

\bigoplus_{i+j=n}\left(\bigoplus_{k+l=j}M_{i,k,l}\right)\to\bigoplus_{i+k+l=n}M_{i,k,l}.

(4)

The morphism
\lambda_{\xi,X}:\mathbb{I}_{\xi}\otimes_{\xi}X\to X
levelwise by
(\lambda_{\xi}(X))^{j}:=\lambda(X^{j})
.

(5)

And the morphism
\rho_{\xi,X}:X\otimes_{\xi}\mathbb{I}_{\xi}\to X
levelwise by
(\rho_{\xi}(X))^{j}:=\rho(X^{j})
.

Remark 4.7
.

Regarding Definition
4.6
the following remarks are to be made:

(1)

The construction of
\alpha_{\xi,X,Y,Z}
can be seen more intuitively in the following diagram in
\mathcal{C}
, which also explains the numerous assumptions on the monoidal structure of
\mathcal{C}
:

\bigoplus\limits_{i+j=n}\left(X^{i}\otimes\left(\bigoplus\limits_{k+l=j}Y^{k}\otimes Z^{l}\right)\right)
\bigoplus\limits_{i+j=n}\left(\bigoplus\limits_{k+l=j}X^{i}\otimes\left(Y^{k}\otimes Z^{l}\right)\right)
\bigoplus\limits_{i+k+l=n}X^{i}\otimes\left(Y^{k}\otimes Z^{l}\right)
\bigoplus\limits_{i+k+l=n}\left(X^{i}\otimes Y^{k}\right)\otimes Z^{l}
\bigoplus\limits_{m+l=n}\left(\bigoplus\limits_{i+k=m}\left(X^{i}\otimes Y^{k}\right)\otimes Z^{l}\right)
\bigoplus\limits_{m+l=n}\left(\left(\bigoplus\limits_{i+k=m}X^{i}\otimes Y^{k}\right)\otimes Z^{l}\right)
.
\bigoplus\limits_{i+j=n}\tau^{-1}_{X^{i},Y^{k}\otimes Z^{l}}
\gamma_{i,k,l}
\bigoplus\limits_{i+k+l=n}\alpha_{X^{i},Y^{k},Z^{l}}
\gamma_{l,i,k}^{-1}
\bigoplus\limits_{m+l=n}\sigma_{X^{i}\otimes Y^{k},Z^{l}}

(2)

By Definition
4.3
the morphisms
\lambda_{\xi,X}
and
\rho_{\xi,X}
are well defined.

(3)

The restriction on
\mathcal{C}
being enriched over some ring can be easily dropped, since if
\mathcal{C}
is just an additive category, it is already enriched over
\mathbb{Z}
. In this case however, one restricts oneself to
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Using these remarks we can prove that we obtain a monoidal structure on the category of
\infty
-complexes. This will already give us a monoidal structure on the category of
N
-complexes if
(\mathcal{C},N,\xi)
allows the twisted Leibniz rule.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbb{I},\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
be a countable distributive
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
-monoidal category for
R
a commutative ring and
\xi\in R
. Then the morphisms from Definition
4.6
define natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\alpha_{\xi}:(\_\otimes_{\xi}\_)\otimes_{\xi}\_

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\_\otimes_{\xi}(\_\otimes_{\xi}\_)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\xi}:(\mathbb{I}_{\xi}\otimes_{\xi}\_)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle\rho_{\xi}:(\_\otimes_{\xi}\mathbb{I}_{\xi})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}.

Proof.

Every morphism involved in Definition
4.6
is a natural isomorphism, so all of the above morphisms define levelwise natural isomorphisms. Since
\mathcal{C}
is a
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
-monoidal category, these natural isomorphisms commute with
\xi
and hence also with the respective differentials, so they define natural isomorphisms.
∎

The proof of the following theorem is a bit sketchy. However, it is really straight forward, but since we have to deal with countable coproducts combined with the pentagon identity and the triangle identity, a rigid proof would take some pages of diagrams which do not give additional insight.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbb{I},\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
be a countable distributive
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
-monoidal category for
R
a commutative ring and
\xi\in R
. Then
(\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C}),\otimes_{\xi},\mathbb{I}_{\xi},\alpha_{\xi},\lambda_{\xi},\rho_{\xi})
defines a monoidal structure on
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
which is countable distributive.

Proof.

All of the above structure morphisms are natural isomorphisms between the corresponding functors by Proposition
4.8
. Hence the only thing left to check are the triangle and the pentagon identities. By Proposition
4.8
and Remark
4.7
we have natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\bigoplus\limits_{i+j=n}\left(X^{i}\otimes\left(\bigoplus\limits_{k+l=j}Y^{k}\otimes Z^{l}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus\limits_{\mathclap{i+k+l=n}}X^{i}\otimes\left(Y^{k}\otimes Z^{l}\right)

\displaystyle\bigoplus\limits_{\mathclap{i+k+l=n}}\left(X^{i}\otimes Y^{k}\right)\otimes Z^{l}

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus\limits_{m+l=n}\left(\left(\bigoplus\limits_{i+k=m}X^{i}\otimes Y^{k}\right)\otimes Z^{l}\right).

So it suffices if the pentagon and triangle identities are satisfied on summands of the form
X^{i}\otimes\left(Y^{k}\otimes Z^{l}\right)
. There they are clearly satisfied, since
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbb{I},\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
is a monoidal category and

\mathbb{I}_{\xi}^{j}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{I}\text{ if }j=0,\\
0\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Now for countable distributivity observe first of all that by Proposition
1.10
the category
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
admits countable coproducts and is additive by Corollary
1.3
. Furthermore, since
\_\otimes_{\xi}\_
is defined levelwise as a coproduct in a countable distributive monoidal category, we get that
\_\otimes_{\xi}\_
is levelwise countable distributive and hence countable distributive since coproducts are computed levelwise.
∎

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbb{I},\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
be a countable distributive
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
-monoidal category, for
R
an integral domain,
\xi\in R
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
(\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C}),\otimes_{\xi},\mathbb{I}_{\xi},\alpha_{\xi},\lambda_{\xi},\rho_{\xi})
is a countable distributive monoidal category if the triple
(\mathcal{C},N,\xi)
allows the twisted Leibniz rule (cf. Definition
3.9
).

Proof.

Since by Proposition
4.9

(\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C}),\otimes_{\xi},\mathbb{I}_{\xi},\alpha_{\xi},\lambda_{\xi},\rho_{\xi})
is a countable distributive monoidal category it suffices to check in all cases, that for
X,Y\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
the complex
X\otimes_{\xi}Y
is again an
N
-complex. But this is just the statement of Proposition
3.11
, since
\_\otimes\_
is in all cases a bifunctor and
(\mathcal{C},N,\xi)
allows the twisted Leibniz rule.
∎

4.2.
Closed monoidal structure

Since most interesting categories are not just monoidal but actually closed monoidal, we also want to lift the adjunctions needed for the structure of a closed monoidal category and an analogous structure on
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{C})
for
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
For this we will use again the liftings we constructed in Section 3 . However, before we can define the internal hom we need to make the following general observation.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
be a bifunctor, such that for all
X\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
the functor
F(\_,X)
admits a right adjoint
G(X,\_):\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
. Then
G(X,\_)
induces a bifunctor

G(\_,\_):\mathcal{C}^{\prime op}\times\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}.

Proof.

The functoriality in the right variable is given by assumption, hence it just remains to be shown that a morphism
f:X\to X^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
induces naturally a morphism

G(f,Y):G(X^{\prime},Y)\to G(X,Y).

For this consider the following composition:

\displaystyle F(G(X^{\prime},Y),X)\xrightarrow{F(\mathrm{Id},f)}F(G(X^{\prime},Y),X^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\epsilon}Y,

where
\epsilon
is the counit of the adjunction. This composition is adjoint to a morphism

G(f,Y):G(X^{\prime},Y)\to G(X,Y),

which is by construction compatible with the structure of
G(X,\_):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
.
∎

Now using this observation we can lift both functors involved in the structure of a closed monoidal structure, and show that these are still compatible in the desired way if we consider modules over a ring.

We conjecture that the following statement holds in general for a closed countable distributive
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
-monoidal category. However the constructions involving
\mathrm{Id}_{X}\otimes d_{Y}
and
[d_{Y},\mathrm{Id}_{Z}]
blow up in the general case. Hence we will only prove the case of modules over a ring
R
, since there the proof is less confusing and one has good control of the adjoints.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and
\xi\in R
. Then we have an adjunction

\_\otimes_{\xi}X:\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\leftrightarrow\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}):[X,\_]_{\xi}.

Here

[\_,\_]_{\xi}:\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})^{op}\times\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\to\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})

is the functor induced by
[\_,\_]:\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{op}\otimes_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
and Definition
3.15
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof we denote by
\mathrm{Gr}(\mathcal{C})
the category of graded objects in a category
\mathcal{C}
.

First observe that we already have in
\mathrm{Gr}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
an isomorphism

\mathrm{Gr}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(X\otimes_{\xi}Y,Z)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Gr}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(X,[Y,Z]_{\xi})

by Definition
3.13
and the tensor-hom adjunction in
\mathrm{Gr}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
.
So it suffices to check that this isomorphism preserves and reflects that a morphism commutes with the differentials.
For this consider a morphism
f\in\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})(X\otimes_{\xi}Y,Z)
. In particular
f\in\mathrm{Gr}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(X\otimes_{\xi}Y,Z)
and
f
has the property

f\circ d_{X\otimes Y}-d_{Z}\circ f=0.

Evaluation of this map at an element
x\otimes y\in X^{i}\otimes Y^{n-i}\subset X\otimes Y
yields now

\displaystyle(f\circ d_{X\otimes Y}-d_{Y}\circ f)(x\otimes y)

\displaystyle=f(d_{X}(x)\otimes y+\xi^{i}x\otimes d_{Y}(y))-d_{Z}(f(x\otimes y))

\displaystyle=f(d_{X}(x)\otimes y)+\xi^{i}f(x\otimes d_{Y}(y))-d_{Z}(f(x\otimes y)).

This is adjoint to the map defined levelwise as

\displaystyle X^{i}\to

\displaystyle[Y,Z]_{\xi}^{i+1}

\displaystyle x\mapsto

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}Y^{n}\to Z^{n+i}\hfill\\
\;\;\;y\mapsto f(d_{X}(x)\otimes y)+\xi^{i}f(x\otimes d_{Y}(y))-d_{Z}(f(x\otimes y))\end{pmatrix}

since the adjunction in
\mathrm{Gr}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
is an adjunction of additive functors.
Now consider the adjoint map
\widehat{f}:X\to[Y,Z]_{\xi}
of
f
. Levelwise this is the map

\displaystyle X^{i}

\displaystyle\to[Y,Z]_{\xi}^{i+1}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}Y\to Z\hfill\\
\;y\mapsto f(x\otimes y)\end{pmatrix}.

Precomposing with
d_{X}
yields that the map
\widehat{f}\circ d_{X}
is levelwise of the form

\displaystyle X^{i}\to

\displaystyle[Y,Z]_{\xi}^{i+1}

\displaystyle x\mapsto

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}Y^{n}\to Z^{n+i}\hfill\\
\;\;\;y\mapsto f(d_{X}(x)\otimes y)\end{pmatrix},

and postcomposing with
d_{[Y,Z]}
gives that the levelwise structure of
d_{[Y,Z]}\circ\widehat{f}
is

\displaystyle X^{i}\to

\displaystyle[Y,Z]_{\xi}^{i+1}

\displaystyle x\mapsto

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}Y^{n}\to Z^{n+i}\hfill\\
\;\;\;y\mapsto d_{Z}(f(x\otimes y))-\xi^{i}f(x\otimes d_{Y}(y))\end{pmatrix}.

Now just as above,
\widehat{f}
is actually a morphism of
\infty
-complexes if and only if

0=\widehat{f}\circ d_{X}-d_{[Y,Z]}\circ\widehat{f}.

After evaluating at an
x\in X^{i}
, this is just the map

\displaystyle X^{i}\to

\displaystyle[Y,Z]_{\xi}^{i+1}

\displaystyle x\mapsto

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}Y^{n}\to Z^{n+i}\hfill\\
\;\;\;y\mapsto f(d_{X}(x)\otimes y)+\xi^{i}f(x\otimes d_{Y}(y))-d_{Z}(f(x\otimes y))\end{pmatrix}.

Hence the adjoint map of
d_{Z}\circ f-f\circ d_{X\otimes_{\xi}Y}
and
\widehat{f}\circ d_{Z}-d_{[Y,Z]_{\xi}}\circ\widehat{f}
have to coincide.
So we may conclude, using that the adjunction is an adjunction between pointed functors, that
f
is a morphism of
\infty
-complexes if and only if
\widehat{f}
is a morphism of
\infty
-complexes.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
R
be an integral domain and
\xi\in R
, then we have an adjunction

\_\otimes_{\xi}X:\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\leftrightarrow\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}):[X,\_]_{\xi}

if
(\mathrm{Mod}_{R},N,\xi)
allows the twisted Leibniz rule.

In particular we get that
(\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}),\otimes_{\xi},\mathbb{I}_{\xi},\alpha_{\xi},\lambda_{\xi},\rho_{\xi})
is a closed monoidal category if
(\mathrm{Mod}_{R},N,\xi)
allows the twisted Leibniz rule.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.12
it just remains to show that
\_\otimes_{\xi}\_
and
[\_,\_]_{\xi}
induce functors of
N
-complexes in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, which is just Corollary
4.10
and Theorem
3.15
.
∎

5.
Frobenius exact structure on
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})

Similar to the the chain complex case one can construct a Frobenius exact structure on the category of
N
-complexes in an additive category
\mathcal{A}
. We will use this to define the homotopy category of
N
-complexes over
\mathcal{A}
as the stable category with respect to this exact structure. This yields immediately that the homotopy category of
N
-complexes is triangulated by Happel’s Theorem. In particular the work done here repeats arguments by Iyama, Kato and Miyashi from their paper
[
12
]
thoroughly. For the notions of exact and Frobenius exact categories we refer to
[
3
]
and Appendix A in the paper by B. Keller
[
14
]
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then define
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
to be the collection of all levelwise split short exact sequences in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
, i. e. sequences which are levelwise isomorphic to a sequence of the form
