\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We will show that
S_{\oplus}
defines a Frobenius exact structure, hence we need to show the following three properties of
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
:

(1)

\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
is an exact structure,

(2)

\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
has enough
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective objects and
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective objects,

(3)

the
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective objects are precisely the
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective objects.

First we show that it indeed is an exact structure:

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
defines an exact structure on
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

Observe that
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
defines levelwise an exact structure. Now direct sums, pushouts and pullbacks in
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
are computed pointwise. Hence
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
defines an exact structure on
\mathrm{C}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
and also on
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
, since it is a full subcategory.
∎

To see that
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
has enough
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective objects and
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective objects we will construct for an
N
-complex
X
an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective object
\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
and an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective object
\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
such that we have an admissible monomorphisms
X\to\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
and an admissible epimorphism
\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)\to X
. The construction of these is motivated by the case of chain complex, which can be recovered from the given construction by setting
N=2
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
X\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then define

(1)

the
N
-complex

\mathrm{I}_{N}(X):=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu^{i}_{N}(X^{i}),

(2)

the
N
-complex

\mathrm{P}_{N}(X):=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu^{i+N-1}_{N}(X^{i}),

(3)

and the morphisms
\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}}:X\to\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
and
\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}}:\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)\to X
by setting levelwise

(\lambda^{\mathrm{I}}_{X})^{i}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i}}&d_{X}&d_{X}^{2}&\cdots&d_{X}^{N-2}&d_{X}^{N-1}\end{bmatrix}^{T}

and

(\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}})^{i}:=\begin{bmatrix}d_{X}^{N-1}&d_{X}^{N-2}&\cdots&d_{X}^{2}&d_{X}&\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i-N+1}}\end{bmatrix}.

Since the data of an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective hull and an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective cover contain the morphisms as well, we check that these are morphisms of
N
-complexes.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the maps
\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}}:X\to\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
and
\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}}:\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)\to X
define morphisms of
N
-complexes.

Proof.

We check separately:

\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}}
:

One needs to check that
d_{\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)}\circ\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}}=\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}}\circ d_{X}
. we calculate

\displaystyle(d_{\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)}\circ\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}})^{i}

\displaystyle=d_{\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)}\circ\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i}}&d_{X}&d_{X}^{2}&\cdots&d_{X}^{N-2}&d_{X}^{N-1}\end{bmatrix}^{T}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}d_{X}&d_{X}^{2}&d_{X}^{3}&\cdots&d_{X}^{N-1}&0\end{bmatrix}^{T}

and

\displaystyle(\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}}\circ d_{X})^{i}=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i+1}}&d_{X}&d_{X}^{2}&\cdots&d_{X}^{N-2}&d_{X}^{N-1}\end{bmatrix}^{T}\circ d_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}d_{X}&d_{X}^{2}&d_{X}^{3}\cdots&d_{X}^{N-1}&d_{X}^{N}\end{bmatrix}^{T}.

So by
d_{X}^{N}=0
the claim holds.

\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}}
:

One needs to check again that
d_{X}\circ\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}}=\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}}\circ d_{\mathrm{P}(X)}.
Now calculation yields

\displaystyle(d_{X}\circ\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}})^{i}

\displaystyle=d_{X}\circ\begin{bmatrix}d_{X}^{N-1}&d_{X}^{N-2}&\cdots&d_{X}^{2}&d_{X}&\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i-N+1}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}d_{X}^{N}&d_{X}^{N-1}&\cdots&d_{X}^{3}&d_{X}^{2}&d_{X}\end{bmatrix}

and

\displaystyle(\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}}\circ d_{\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)})^{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}d_{X}^{N-1}&d_{X}^{N-2}&\cdots&d_{X}^{2}&d_{X}&\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i-N+1}}\end{bmatrix}\circ d_{\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&d_{X}^{N-1}&\cdots&d_{X}^{3}&d_{X}^{2}&d_{X}\end{bmatrix}.

This again concludes the proof since
d_{X}^{N}=0
.∎

Now we also check that
\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
and
\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
define an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-cover and
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-hull respectively.

Proposition 5.5
.

There are sequences in
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
of the form

\hbox to185.8pt{\vbox to25.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-9.53151pt\lower-3.21124pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 82.03 7.09 L 116.89 7.09}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{84.67435pt}{5.12167pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

and

\hbox to185.8pt{\vbox to25.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-9.53151pt\lower-3.21124pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

{{{{{}}{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 83.25 7.09 L 114.68 7.09}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{83.07715pt}{5.12167pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{65.34244pt}{10.15468pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 168.23 7.09 L 200.88 7.09}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{145.37558pt}{5.12167pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Proof.

This becomes obvious if we consider
Y=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu^{i-1}_{N-1}(X^{i})
in the first sequence and
Y^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu^{i+N}_{N-1}(X^{i})
in the second.
∎

To check that
\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
and
\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
really are
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective (resp.
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective) objects, we need to show by the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective (resp.
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective) objects that every sequence in
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
such that the rightmost term is
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective, or the leftmost term is
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective admits a splitting in the category of
N
-complexes. For this we will explicitly construct the splittings and prove that they indeed are morphisms of
N
-complexes.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have

(1)

\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
is
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective,

(2)

\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
is
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective.

Proof.

We will prove in both cases that if we have a levelwise splitting morphism
\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)\to Y
respectively
Y\to\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
that this morphism already admits a splitting in the
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.

(1)

Let
f:\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)\to Y
be a morphism of
N
-complexes such that there exists for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
a levelwise split
h^{i}:Y^{i}\to\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)^{i}
, satisfying
h^{i}\circ f^{i}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)}
. We need to provide a morphism of
N
-complexes
g:Y\to\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
such that
g\circ f=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)}
.

The
f^{i}
and
h^{i}
morphisms are by definition of the form

f^{i}=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}^{i}&f_{2}^{i}&\cdots&f_{N}^{i}\end{bmatrix}\;,\;h^{i}=\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}^{i}&h_{2}^{i}&\cdots&h_{N}^{i}\end{bmatrix}^{T},

now the fact that
h^{i}\circ f^{i}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)^{i}}
just means
h_{j}^{i}\circ f_{j}^{i}=\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i+j}}
and
h_{j}^{i}\circ f_{k}^{i}=0
if
k\neq j
. Since
f
is a morphism of
N
-complexes, we know that
d_{Y}\circ f^{i}=f^{i+1}\circ d_{\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)}
, but this just implies, that
d_{Y}\circ f_{j}^{i}=f_{j-1}^{i+1}
if
1<j\leq N
and zero otherwise. Furthermore, observe that by the definition of
\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
the following equation holds for
2\leq j\leq N
:

h_{j}^{i}\circ f_{j}^{i}=\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i+j}}=h_{j-1}^{i+1}\circ f_{j-1}^{i+1}.

Now define the morphism
g:Y\to\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
levelwise as

g^{i}:=\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}^{i}&h_{1}^{i+1}\circ d_{Y}&\cdots&h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}\end{bmatrix}^{T}.

Note that this
g
is well defined by the definition of
\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
. Furthermore this indeed defines a morphism of
N
-complexes since

\displaystyle(g\circ d_{Y})^{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}^{i+1}\circ d_{Y}&h_{1}^{i+2}\circ d_{Y}^{2}&\cdots&h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}&h_{1}^{i+N}\circ d_{Y}^{N}\end{bmatrix}^{T}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}^{i+1}\circ d_{Y}&h_{1}^{i+2}\circ d_{Y}^{2}&\cdots&h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}&0\end{bmatrix}^{T}

\displaystyle=(d_{\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)}\circ g)^{i}.

The morphism
g
is a section in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
, since

\displaystyle g\circ f

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}^{i}\\
h_{1}^{i+1}\circ d_{Y}\\
h_{1}^{i+2}\circ d_{Y}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}]^{T}\end{bmatrix}\circ\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}^{i}&f_{2}^{i}&f_{3}^{i}&...&f_{N}^{i}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}^{i}\circ f_{1}^{i}&h_{1}^{i}\circ f_{2}^{i}&h_{1}^{i}\circ f_{3}^{i}&\cdots&h^{i}_{1}\circ f^{i}_{N}\\
h_{1}^{i+1}\circ d_{Y}\circ f_{1}^{i}&h_{1}^{i+1}\circ d_{Y}\circ f_{2}^{i}&h_{1}^{i+1}\circ d_{Y}\circ f_{3}^{i}&\cdots&h_{1}^{i+1}\circ d_{Y}\circ f_{N}^{i}\\
h_{1}^{i+2}\circ d_{Y}^{2}\circ f_{1}^{i}&h_{1}^{i+2}\circ d_{Y}^{2}\circ f_{2}^{i}&h_{1}^{i+2}\circ d_{Y}^{2}\circ f_{3}^{i}&\cdots&h_{1}^{i+2}\circ d_{Y}^{2}\circ f_{N}^{i}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ f_{1}^{i}&h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ f_{2}^{i}&h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ f_{3}^{i}&\cdots&h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ f_{N}^{i}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}^{i}\circ f_{1}^{i}&h_{1}^{i}\circ f_{2}^{i}&h_{1}^{i}\circ f_{3}^{i}&\cdots&h^{i}_{1}\circ f^{i}_{N}\\
h_{1}^{i+1}\circ 0&h_{1}^{i+1}\circ f_{1}^{i+1}&h_{1}^{i+1}\circ f_{2}^{i+1}&\cdots&h_{1}^{i+1}\circ f_{N-1}^{i+1}\\
h_{1}^{i+2}\circ 0&h_{1}^{i+2}\circ 0&h_{1}^{i+2}\circ f_{3}^{i+2}&\cdots&h_{1}^{i+2}\circ f_{N-2}^{i+2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ 0&h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ 0&h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ 0&\cdots&h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ f_{1}^{i+N-1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}^{i}\circ f_{1}^{i}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&h_{1}^{i+1}\circ f_{1}^{i+1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&h_{1}^{i+2}\circ f_{1}^{i+2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&h_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ f_{1}^{i+N-1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i}}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i+1}}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i+2}}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&\mathrm{Id}_{X}{{}^{i+N-1}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)}^{i},

where we use in the third to last row that
d_{Y}\circ f_{j}^{i}=f_{j-1}^{i+1}
and
d_{Y}\circ f_{1}^{i}=0
. In the second to last row we used
f_{j}^{i}\circ h_{k}^{i}=0
for
k\neq j
.

(2)

This case is just the dual of (1), but for completeness we give the proof as well.

Let
f:Y\to\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
be a morphism of
N
-complexes such that there exists for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
a levelwise split
h^{i}:\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)^{i}\to Y^{i}
, satisfying
f^{i}\circ h^{i}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)}^{i}
, we now need to provide a morphism of
N
-complexes
g:\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)\to Y
such that
f\circ g=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{P}(X)}
.

Again the morphisms
f^{i}
and
h^{i}
are of the form

f^{i}=\begin{bmatrix}f^{i}_{1}&f_{2}&f^{i}_{3}&\cdots&f^{i}_{N}\end{bmatrix}^{T}\;,\;h^{i}=\begin{bmatrix}h^{i}_{1}&h^{i}_{2}&h^{i}_{3}&\cdots&h^{i}_{N}\end{bmatrix}.

The fact that
f^{i}\circ h^{i}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)^{i}}
yields
f_{j}^{i}\circ h_{j}^{i}=\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i-N+j+1}}
and
f_{j}^{i}\circ h_{k}^{i}=0
if
k\neq j
. Since
f
is a morphism of
N
-complexes, we know that
f^{i}\circ d_{Y}=d_{\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)}\circ f^{i+1}
, but this implies that
f_{j}^{i}\circ d_{Y}=f_{j-1}^{i+1}
if
1<j\leq N
and zero otherwise. Furthermore, observe that by the definition of
\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
the following equation holds for
2\leq j\leq N
:

f_{j}^{i}\circ h_{j}^{i}=\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i-N+j}}=f_{j-1}^{i+1}\circ h_{j-1}^{i+1}.

Hence define
g:\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)\to Y
dually to (1) as

g^{i}:=\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}^{i}&d_{Y}\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&\cdots&d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\end{bmatrix}.

Note that this
g
is again well defined by the definition of
\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
, and defines a morphism of
N
-complexes since

\displaystyle(d_{Y}\circ g)^{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}d_{Y}\circ h_{1}^{i-1}&d_{Y}^{2}\circ h_{1}^{i}&\cdots&d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-3}&d_{Y}^{N}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}d_{Y}\circ h_{1}^{i-1}&d_{Y}^{2}\circ h_{1}^{i}&\cdots&d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-3}&0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=(g\circ d_{\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)})^{i}.

the morphism
g
is a section in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
, since

\displaystyle f\circ g

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}^{i}\\
f_{2}^{i}\\
f_{3}^{i}\\
\vdots\\
f_{N}^{i}\end{bmatrix}\circ\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}^{i}&d_{Y}\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&d_{Y}^{2}\circ h_{1}^{i+2}&...&d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}^{i}\circ h_{1}^{i}&f_{1}^{i}\circ d_{Y}\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&f_{1}^{i}\circ d_{Y}^{2}\circ h_{1}^{i+2}&\cdots&f^{i}_{1}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\\
f_{2}^{i}\circ h_{1}^{i}&f_{2}^{i}\circ d_{Y}\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&f_{2}^{i}\circ d_{Y}^{2}\circ h_{1}^{i+2}&\cdots&f_{2}^{i}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\\
f_{3}^{i}\circ h_{1}^{i}&f_{3}^{i}\circ d_{Y}\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&f_{3}^{i}\circ d_{Y}^{2}\circ h_{1}^{i+2}&\cdots&f_{3}^{i}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
f_{N}^{i}\circ h_{1}^{i}&f_{N}^{i}\circ d_{Y}\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&f_{N}^{i}\circ d_{Y}^{2}\circ h_{1}^{i+2}&\cdots&f_{N}^{i}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}^{i}\circ h_{1}^{i}&0\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&0\circ h_{1}^{i+2}&\cdots&0\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\\
f_{2}^{i}\circ h_{1}^{i}&f_{1}^{i+1}\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&0\circ h_{1}^{i+2}&\cdots&0\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\\
f_{3}^{i}\circ h_{1}^{i}&f_{2}^{i+1}\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&f_{1}^{i+2}\circ h_{1}^{i+2}&\cdots&0\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
f_{N}^{i}\circ h_{1}^{i}&f_{N}^{i}\circ d_{Y}\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&f_{N}^{i}\circ d_{Y}^{2}\circ h_{1}^{i+2}&...&f_{N}^{i}\circ d_{Y}^{N-1}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}^{i}\circ h_{1}^{i}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&f_{1}^{i+1}\circ h_{1}^{i+1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&f_{1}^{i+2}\circ h_{1}^{i+2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&...&f_{1}^{i+N-1}\circ h_{1}^{i+N-1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i}-N+j}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i-N+1}}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\mathrm{Id}_{X^{i-N+2}}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&\mathrm{Id}_{X}{{}^{i}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)}^{i},

where we use in the third to last row
f_{j}^{i}\circ d_{Y}=f_{j-1}^{i+1}
and
f_{1}^{i}\circ d_{Y}=0
. In the second to last row we use
f_{j}^{i}\circ h_{k}^{i}=0
for
k\neq j
.

∎

Now the last thing we need for
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
to be a Frobenius exact structure on
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is that
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injectives and
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projectives coincide.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then an object in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is
S_{\oplus}
-projective if and only if it is
S_{\oplus}
-injective.

Proof.

Let
P
be
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective, then we have a sequence in
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
of the form:

Now since
P
is
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective, this short exact sequence splits, hence
\mathrm{P}_{N}(P)\cong P\oplus X
. But since
\mathrm{P}_{N}(P)=\mathrm{I}_{N}(P[-N+1])
we have that
P
is a direct summand of an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective object, and hence
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective itself. Here
P[-N+1]
denotes the
N
-complex of
P
shifted by
-N+1
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Now again the short exact sequence splits since
I
is
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective, and we get
\mathrm{I}_{N}(I)\cong I\oplus X
. Now
\mathrm{I}_{N}(I)=\mathrm{P}_{N}(I[N-1])
and hence
I
is a summand of an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective object and hence itself
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective.
∎

Now we can put together the above properties of
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
to get:

Theorem 5.8
.

For an additive category
\mathcal{A}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
the exact structure
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
defines a Frobenius exact structure on the category
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.2
, The collection
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
defines an exact structure. It has enough
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projectives and
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injectives, as any object admits an admissible monomorphism
\lambda^{\mathrm{I}}_{X}:X\to\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
and an admissible epimorphism
\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}}:\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)\to X
by Proposition
5.5
and Proposition
5.6
. Finally
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective objects are precisely the
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-projective objects by Proposition
5.7
. So all defining criteria of Frobenius exactness are fulfilled.
∎

Having established this Frobenius exact structure we define the homotopy category of
N
-complexes and use Happel’s Theorem I.2.6
[
10
]
to conclude that the homotopy category is well defined and triangulated in a canonical way.

Remark 5.9
.

We would like to remind that by 13.4 in
[
3
]
the stable category
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{S}}
of a Frobenius exact category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{S}}
is defined to have the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
and for
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
the morphism space
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{S}}(X,Y):=\mathcal{C}(X,Y)/I
, where
I
is the two-sided ideal of morphisms factoring through an
\mathcal{S}
-injective-projective object.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then denote by
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
the stable category of
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})_{S_{\oplus}}
.

Corollary 5.11
.

The category
\mathrm{K}_{N}(A)
is triangulated for
\mathcal{A}
an additive category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Furthermore every
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-short exact sequence

0
X
Y
Z
0
f
g

induces a triangle

X
Y
Z
\Sigma X
f
g
h

and every triangle is isomorphic to a triangle of this form.

Proof.

This is a direct application of Happel’s Theorem I.2.6
[
10
]
.
∎

From now on we will often need that every morphism can be completed into a triangle in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. We will refer to a completion of a morphism as its cone. Since we do not need any specific structure of these cones we just fix any choice of completion.

Definition 5.12
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
for
\mathcal{A}
an additive category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we refer to
the cone of
f
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
as
\mathrm{Cone}_{K_{N}(\mathcal{A})}(f)
, or just
\mathrm{Cone}(f)
if it is clear from context. This is just any choice of completion of the morphism into a triangle

For a precise construction of the cone objects together with the defining morphisms arising by Happel’s Theorem we refer the reader to “Derived categories of
N
-complexes”
[
12
]
.

Remark 5.13
.

By the definition of a triangulated category these completions are unique up to non-unique isomorphism.

6.
Properties of the homotopy category of
N
-complexes

Since a goal of this work is to imitate the definition of the derived category of an abelian category, we will need some general properties of the homotopy category
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. This will give us more control over the objects, morphisms and coproducts in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.

First of all we will need to be able to factor through specific
\mathcal{S}
-injective and
\mathcal{S}
-projective objects. This is possible for any exact structure which has enough projectives and injectives respectively. Using this property we will be able to show that coproducts in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
can be calculated naively and prove a more calculation based approach towards the homotopy category.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{S}}
be an exact category. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

If
X\in\mathcal{C}
admits an
\mathcal{S}
-injective hull
X\xrightarrow{\lambda_{X}}I_{X}
we have that a morphism
f:X\to Y
factors as
f:X\to I\to Y
, where
I
is an
\mathcal{S}
-injective object, if and only if
f
factors as
f:X\xrightarrow{\lambda_{X}}I_{X}\to Y
.

(2)

If
Y\in\mathcal{C}
admits an
\mathcal{S}
-projective resolution
P_{Y}\xrightarrow{\lambda_{Y}}Y
we have that a morphism
f:X\to Y
factors as
f:X\to P\to Y
, where
P
is an
\mathcal{S}
-projective object, if and only if
f
factors as
f:X\to P_{Y}\xrightarrow{\lambda_{Y}}Y
.

Proof.

(1)

The if part is clear, since if
f:X\to Y
factors over a chosen
\mathcal{S}
-injective, it especially factors over an
\mathcal{S}
-injective object.

For the only if part, let
f:X\xrightarrow{\lambda}I\xrightarrow{g}Y
be a factorisation of a morphism
f
such that
I
is
\mathcal{S}
-injective. Now consider the following diagram in
\mathcal{C}
:

Y
I
X
I_{X}
Z
M
Z
\ulcorner
,
f
\lambda
\lambda_{X}
g
h
j

where the top sequence is induced by the
\mathcal{S}
-injective hull
I_{X}
for
X
. The left square is a pushout, which we can form since
(X\xrightarrow{\lambda_{X}}I_{X}\to Z)\in\mathcal{S}
.
Since the top sequence is in
\mathcal{S}
we get by the stability of exact structures under pushouts that also the bottom one is in
\mathcal{S}
. The bottom sequence now splits since
I
is
\mathcal{S}
-injective by assumption. Call this split
s:M\to I
. Hence we get the diagram in
\mathcal{C}
:

Y
I
X
I_{X}
Z
M
Z
\ulcorner
.
f
\lambda
\lambda_{X}
g
h
j
s

Using this to factor
f
we get the claimed factorization:

f=g\circ\lambda=g\circ s\circ j\circ\lambda=g\circ s\circ h\circ\lambda_{X}.

(2)

Again the if part is clear since a specific choice of an
\mathcal{S}
-projective object in particular gives us an
\mathcal{S}
-projective object.
For the only if part consider again a morphism
f:X\to Y
which factors as
f:X\xrightarrow{g}P\xrightarrow{\lambda}Y
for some
\mathcal{S}
-projective object
P
. Now consider the
\mathcal{S}
-projective resolution
P_{Y}\xrightarrow{\lambda_{Y}}Y
, and the following diagram in
\mathcal{C}
:

Y
P
X
P_{Y}
Z
M
Z
\lrcorner
,
f
\lambda
\lambda_{Y}
g
h
j

where the right square is a pullback, which we can form since the lower sequence is in
\mathcal{S}
. Now we get again using that
\mathcal{S}
is an exact structure, that the top row is in
\mathcal{S}
. So the fact that
P
is
\mathcal{S}
-projective yields that
j
splits. Call this split
s:P\to M
. This means that we get the diagram:

Y
P
X
P_{Y}
Z
M
Z
\lrcorner
.
f
\lambda
\lambda_{Y}
g
h
j
s

Using this diagram we can calculate

f=\lambda\circ g=\lambda\circ j\circ s\circ g=\lambda_{Y}\circ h\circ s\circ g,

which concludes the proof.∎

As mentioned before we can use this argument to show that
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
admits coproducts and give a precise description of them.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{S}}
be a Frobenius exact category such that
\mathcal{F}
admits
I
-indexed coproducts for
I
a set. Then the stable category
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{S}}
also admits
I
-indexed coproducts and we have that the canonical functor
\mathcal{F}\to\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{S}}
preserves coproducts, i. e. the morphisms exhibiting the coproduct in
\mathcal{F}
are representatives of the morphisms exhibiting the coproduct in
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

We will show that for a coproduct
\left(\bigoplus\limits_{i\in I}X_{i},\iota_{i}\right)
in
\mathcal{F}
the maps
\left[\iota_{i}\right]
exhibit
\bigoplus\limits_{i\in I}X_{i}
as a coproduct in
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

First consider the coproduct
\left(\bigoplus\limits_{i\in I}X_{i},\iota_{i}\right)
in
\mathcal{F}
. The
\iota_{i}
induces morphisms
[\iota_{i}]:X_{i}\to\bigoplus\limits_{i\in I}X_{i}
in
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Now consider for each
i\in I
a morphism
\psi_{i}:X_{i}\to T
in
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{S}}
and pick for each of these a representative
\varphi_{i}
such that
[\varphi_{i}]=\psi_{i}
, which are morphisms
\varphi_{i}:X_{i}\to T
in
\mathcal{F}
. So we get by the universal property of the coproduct a unique morphism
\varphi:\bigoplus\limits_{i\in I}X_{i}\to T
, such that the following diagram commutes for each
i\in I
:

X_{i}
\bigoplus X_{i}
T.
\iota_{i}
\varphi_{i}
\varphi

This implies by the definition of the stable category that the same diagram commutes in
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Hence it just remains to show uniqueness of
\varphi
. Since
\mathcal{F}
is additive it actually suffices to show that if we have a morphism
f:\bigoplus\limits_{i\in I}X_{i}\to T
in
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{S}}
such that all
f\circ[\iota_{i}]
vanish, so does
f
. Hence assume
f\circ[\iota_{i}]=0
and choose representatives
[f_{i}]=f\circ[\iota_{i}]
. These
f_{i}
now factor by definition of the stable category and Proposition
6.1
as
f_{i}=\lambda_{T}\circ\psi_{i}
for some
\psi_{i}:X_{i}\to P_{T}
and
\lambda_{T}:P_{T}\to T
some
\mathcal{S}
-projective resolution of
T
. So we get for all
i\in I
a commutative diagram in
\mathcal{F}

X_{i}
\bigoplus X_{i}
T.
P_{T}
\psi_{i}
\lambda_{T}
\iota_{i}
f_{i}
f

By the universal property of the coproduct this induces a map
\psi:\bigoplus\limits_{i\in I}X_{i}\to P_{T}
, such that the top, the left and the outer triangle commute in the following diagram in
\mathcal{F}
:

X_{i}
\bigoplus X_{i}
T.
P_{T}
\psi
\psi_{i}
\lambda_{T}
\iota_{i}
f_{i}
f

This implies that the whole diagram commutes. The commutativity of this diagram now just means
[f]=0
in
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{S}}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

One can show an analogous statement for products. There one just needs to use
\mathcal{S}
-injective hulls instead of
\mathcal{S}
-projective resolutions. However this is not important for the rest of our constructions, and so we omit it.

We are also interested in having a more calculation based approach towards the structure of the homotopy category.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of
N
-complexes in
\mathcal{A}
. Then
f
is said to be
nullhomotopic
if there exists for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
an
s^{j}\in\mathcal{A}(X^{j},Y^{j-N+1})
such that for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
we get:

f^{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}d^{N-j-1}\circ s^{i+j}\circ d^{j}.

This definition should of course be equivalent to the factoring of a morphism over an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective-projective object.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category and
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

the morphism
f
is nullhomotopic,

(2)

the morphism
f
factors through an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective-projective object.

Proof.

We show the implications separately:

(1)
\implies
(2):

By definition of a nullhomotopic morphism there exist
s^{t}\in\mathcal{A}(X^{t},Y^{t-N+1})
for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

f^{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}d^{N-j-1}\circ s^{i+j}\circ d^{j}.

Consider the map
\lambda^{I}_{N}:X\to\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
. The maps
s^{i}:X^{i}\to Y^{i-N+1}
induce maps
\hat{s}^{i}:\mu^{i}_{N}(X^{i})^{i}\to Y
via the adjunction
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(\mu_{N}^{i}(X^{i}),Y)\cong\mathcal{A}(X^{i},Y^{i-N+1})
from Proposition
1.8
. But this gives a map
\hat{s}:\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)\to Y
since the source is just a direct sum of
\mu_{N}^{i}(X^{i})
. Unravelling the definition of
\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
,
\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}}
and the adjunction one gets:

\displaystyle(\lambda^{\mathrm{I}}_{X})^{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&d&d^{2}&\cdots&d^{N-2}&d^{N-1}\end{bmatrix}^{T}:X^{i}\to X^{i}\oplus X^{i+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus X^{i+N-2}\oplus X^{i+N-1})

\displaystyle\hat{s}^{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}d^{N-1}\circ s^{i}&d^{N-2}\circ s^{i+1}&d^{N-3}\circ s^{i+2}&\cdots&s^{i+N-1}\end{bmatrix}:X^{i}\oplus X^{i+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus X^{i+N-1}\to Y^{i}).

Composing these morphisms yields now:

(\hat{s}\circ\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}})^{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}d^{N-j-1}s^{i+j}d^{j}=f^{i}.

So since
\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
is
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective-projective this proves the claim.

(2)
\implies
(1):

Let
f:X\to Y
factor as
f=s\circ g
where
g:X\to P
,
s:P\to Y
and
P
is
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective-projective. Then we may assume by Lemma
6.1
that
P\cong\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
and
g=\lambda^{\mathrm{I}}_{X}
. Now the claim follows cf. Remark
6.6
, since we have that
f
factors as
\lambda^{\mathrm{I}}_{X}\circ s
where
\lambda^{\mathrm{I}}_{X}:X\to\mathrm{I}(X)
and
s:\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)\to Y
. More precisely these
s^{i}
now define levelwise maps
s^{i}:X^{i}\oplus X^{i+1}\oplus X^{i+2}...\oplus X^{i+N-1}\to Y^{i}
, the fact that these commute with the differential of
\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
yields

s^{i}\circ\iota_{X^{j}}=s^{i+1}\circ\iota_{X^{j}}\text{ for }0<j-i<N.

Here
\iota_{X^{j}}:X^{j}\hookrightarrow\bigoplus\limits_{l=i}^{\mathclap{i+N-1}}X^{l}
is the canonicalinclusion for
i\leq j\leq i+N-1
.

This means that
s:\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)\to Y
is equivalently defined by
\widehat{s^{i}}:X^{i}\to Y^{i-N+1}
and

s^{i}=\begin{bmatrix}d^{N-1}\circ\widehat{s}^{i}&d^{N-2}\circ\widehat{s}^{i+1}&d^{N-3}\circ\widehat{s}^{i+2}&\cdots&\widehat{s}^{i+N-1}\end{bmatrix}:X^{i}\oplus X^{i+1}\oplus X^{i+2}\cdots\oplus X^{i+N-1}\to Y^{i}.

So we get

(s\circ\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}})^{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}d^{N-j-1}\circ\widehat{s}^{i+j}\circ d^{j}=f^{i},

as claimed. ∎

Remark 6.6
.

The construction in terms of adjunctions can be seen more intuitively using the following diagram in
\mathcal{A}
, in which we only consider one summand of
\mu_{N}^{i}(X)
:

\hbox to413.34pt{\vbox to120.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 57.29156pt\lower-13.51424pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
\par{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-53.95856pt}{-8.19731pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{145.209pt}{-8.19731pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

By considering the composition of the two vertical morphisms one gets the desired summands for the homotopy equation.

7.
Two approaches to the derived category of
N
-complexes

After this work on the homotopy category of
N
-complexes and its properties we are finally able to inspect the notion of cohomology of
N
-complexes. In particular this yields the notions of
N
-quasi-isomorphisms and the derived category of
N
-complexes. As in the case of chain complexes this category is defined as a Verdier quotient of the homotopy category of
N
-complexes. However, in chain complexes over a ring one can realize the derived category already as a subcategory of the homotopy category, namely as the full subcategory of h-projectives.

The aim of this section is to understand the notion of
N
-cohomology and
N
-quasi-isomorphisms and use this in the definition of the derived category of
N
-complexes and the property of being h-projective. Finally we will show that for the category of modules over a ring the full subcategory of the homotopy category consisting of h-projective
N
-complexes already realizes the derived category. To prove this we will use a construction of semifree complexes which is inspired by an argument of Drinfeld
[
5
]
.

Each of the approaches mentioned above has its advantages. For example the construction as a Verdier quotient is more straightforward, intuitive and comes with a canonical functor. On the other hand the construction using h-projectives allows better control over the morphisms in the derived category. This is due to the fact that all morphisms are already contained in the homotopy category and calculations using roof diagrams are not needed.

In Section 8 we will construct another realization of the derived category of
N
-complexes using model structures. The advantage of this is that it shows that the derived category is indeed the Gabriel-Zismann localisation along certain morphisms.

In order to get a first notion of what the derived category of
N
-complexes should be we need to define the cohomology of an
N
-complex. Furthermore we will show that it still defines a functor just as in the usual case. A problem however is that the cohomology of
N
-complexes consists of more than one object in each degree, but we will see that we loose less than one might think due to that.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
X\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then the
r
-th cohomology in degree

i
, for
1\leq r\leq N-1
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, is the following object in
\mathcal{A}
:

H_{(r)}^{i}(X):=\ker(d^{r})^{i}/\mathrm{im}(d^{N-r})^{i}.

Proposition 7.2
.

Definition
7.1
can be extended to an additive functor

H_{(r)}^{i}(\_):\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A}.

Proof.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. By definition
f
commutes with the differentials, hence
f\circ d_{X}^{r}=d_{Y}^{r}\circ f
. So
f
induces a unique map
\ker(d^{r})^{i}(f):\ker(d_{X}^{r})^{i}\to\ker(d^{r}_{Y})^{i}
by the universal property of
\ker(d^{r}_{Y})
such that the diagram

\ker(d_{Y}^{r})^{i}
Y^{i}
Y^{i+r}
\ker(d_{X}^{r})^{i}
f^{i}|_{\ker(d_{Y}^{r})}
d^{r}
\exists!\ker(d^{r})^{i}(f)

commutes, since

d^{r}\circ f|_{\ker(d_{X}^{r})}=f\circ d_{X}^{r}|_{\ker(d_{X}^{r})}=0.

Hence to get a functor from
N
-complexes to the ground category it suffices to check that
f(\mathrm{im}(d_{X}^{N-r}))\subset\mathrm{im}(d_{Y}^{N-r})
, but this is satisfied, since by definition
f
commutes with the differential.

Now it remains to check that this indeed is an additive functor. But for this we only need to check that the assignment
f\mapsto\ker(d^{r})^{i}(f)
is additive. Hence let
f=g+g^{\prime}
. In this case both
\ker(d^{r})^{i}(f)
and
\ker(d^{r})^{i}(g)+\ker(d^{r})^{i}(g^{\prime})
would make the diagram commute, and hence they have to coincide by the universal property of the kernel.
∎

Having defined the notion of cohomology, it is natural to define the notion of an
N
-quasi-isomorphism:

Definition 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. We call a morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
an
N
-quasi-isomorphism
if
H_{(r)}^{i}(f)
is an isomorphism for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq r\leq N-1
.

With these basic definitions we will now show that cohomology defines a functor on the category
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
as well.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
X\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
such that
X\cong 0
in the category
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then
H_{(r)}^{i}(X)=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq r\leq N-1
.

Proof.

The assumption
X\cong 0
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
implies that
\mathrm{Id}_{X}
factors through
\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
. Hence
X
is a direct summand of
\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. So by Proposition
7.2
it is enough to show for all
X\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
that
H^{i}_{(r)}(\mathrm{P}_{N}(X))=0
. Now by the definition of
\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
we have

\mathrm{P}_{N}(X):=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{N}^{j}(X^{j}).

So it suffices to check the claim for
\mu_{N}^{j}(X^{j})
. Now observe that
\ker(d_{\mu_{N}^{j}(X^{j})}^{r})^{i}=X^{j}
if
j-r<i\leq j
, and zero else. So we just need to consider the case
j-r<i\leq j
, but then

\mathrm{im}(d_{\mu_{N}^{j}(X^{j})}^{N-r})^{i}=X^{j}=\ker(d_{\mu_{N}^{j}(X^{j})}^{r})^{i}

and hence
H_{(r)}^{i}(\mu_{N}^{j}(X^{j}))=0
. This finishes the proof since
H^{i}_{(r)}(\_)
is additive.
∎

Corollary 7.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq r\leq N-1
. Then
H_{(r)}^{i}(\_)
induces an additive functor
H_{(r)}^{i}(\_):\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.2
and Proposition
7.4
the functor
H_{(r)}^{i}(\_):\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A}
is additive and sends all morphisms to zero that factor through an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective-projective object. But
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is the stable category of
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})_{\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}}
and hence
H_{(r)}^{i}(\_)
factors through
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.
∎

Corollary 7.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
f:X\to Y
a morphism in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
, such that
f
induces an isomorphism in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then
f
is an
N
-quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

Obvious by Corollary
7.5
.
∎

In the case of
N=2
the long exact sequence in cohomology is a powerful tool to calculate cohomology.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and

0
X
Y
Z
0
f
g

a short exact sequence in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then there exists for every
1\leq r\leq N-1
a long exact sequence of the form:

\cdots
H^{i-N}_{(r)}(Z)
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(X)
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(Y)
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(Z)
H^{i}_{(r)}(X)
H^{i}_{(r)}(Y)
H^{i}_{(r)}(Z)
H^{i+r}_{(r)}(X)
\cdots
.
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(f)
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(g)
H^{i}_{(r)}(f)
H^{i}_{(r)}(g)

Proof.

Fix
1\leq r\leq N-1
. Then we may build a short exact sequence

0
\widehat{X}
\widehat{Y}
\widehat{Z}
0
\widehat{f}
\widehat{g}

in
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\mathcal{A})
by considering the following diagram in
\mathcal{A}
:

\hbox to191.79pt{\vbox to201.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-57.36638pt\lower-13.57576pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Now we get a long exact sequence in ordinary cohomology of the form

\cdots
H^{-2}(\widehat{X})
H^{-1}(\widehat{X})
H^{-1}(\widehat{Y})
H^{-1}(\widehat{Z})
H^{0}(\widehat{X})
H^{0}(\widehat{Y})
H^{0}(\widehat{Z})
H^{1}(\widehat{X})
\cdots
.
H^{-1}(\widehat{f})
H^{-1}(\widehat{g})
H^{0}(\widehat{f})
H^{0}(\widehat{g})

But this is just the desired sequence by the definition of
\widehat{X}
,
\widehat{Y}
and
\widehat{Z}
.
∎

Corollary 7.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and

X
Y
Z
\Sigma(X)
f
g
h

be a triangle in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then this triangle induces long exact sequences like in Lemma
7.7
.

Proof.

The triangle comes by Corollary
5.11
from an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-exact sequence, hence we get the long exact sequence for this short exact sequence by Lemma
7.7
. Since all objects in this short exact sequence are isomorphic in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
to the objects in our triangle we get the statement by Corollary
7.6
.
∎

One of the most important features of the long exact sequence in ordinary cohomology is a characterisation of a
2
-quasi-isomorphism by its cone. This is still possible in the general case.

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then
f
is an
N
-quasi-isomorphism if and only if
H_{(r)}^{i}(\mathrm{Cone}(f))=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq r\leq N-1
.

Proof.

We have a triangle of the form

\hbox to176.88pt{\vbox to24.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-43.3469pt\lower-3.21124pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

hence we get long exact sequences

\cdots
H^{i-N}_{(r)}(\mathrm{Cone}(f))
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(X)
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(Y)
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(\mathrm{Cone}(f))
H^{i}_{(r)}(X)
H^{i}_{(r)}(Y)
H^{i}_{(r)}(\mathrm{Cone}(f))
H^{i+r}_{(r)}(X)
\cdots
.
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(f)
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(g)
H^{i}_{(r)}(f)
H^{i}_{(r)}(g)

But by the exactness of these sequences we get that
H^{i}_{(r)}(f)
is an isomorphism for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq r\leq N-1
, if and only if
H^{i}_{(r)}(\mathrm{Cone}(f))=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq r\leq N-1
.
∎

7.1.
Verdier quotient

Having established these general tools for cohomology of
N
-complexes we can start to construct the derived category in the way of Osamu Iyama, Kiriko Kato and Jun-Ichi Miyachi
[
12
]
. This construction is quite straightforward if one keeps in mind that the objects we want to divide out are the ones with vanishing cohomology. Now it just remains to check that these are stable enough to define a Verdier quotient.

Definition 7.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. We call an object
X\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})

acyclic
, if
H^{i}_{(r)}(X)=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq r\leq N-1
. We refer to the full subcategory of
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
consisting of acyclic objects as
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
is a thick triangulated subcategory of
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

We check the two properties separately:

thick:

Let
X\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
be a direct summand of
Y\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
. Then there exists a
Z
such that
X\oplus Z\cong Y
, but this implies since
H^{i}_{(r)}
is additive for all
1\leq r\leq N
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
that

H^{i}_{(r)}(X)\oplus H^{i}_{(r)}(Z)\cong H^{i}_{(r)}(Y)\cong 0,

hence
H^{i}_{(r)}(X)=0
, so we get
X\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
.

triangulated:

We need to check that for all triangles

X
Y
Z
\Sigma(X)
f
g
h

in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
such that
X
and
Z
are acyclic, also
Y
is acyclic, and that if
X\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
, also
\Sigma(\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A}))
and
\Sigma^{-1}(\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A}))
are objects in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
.

First consider the triangle above, then we get for all
1\leq r\leq N-1
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
by Corollary
7.8
a long exact sequence of the form

\cdots
0
0
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(Y)
0
0
H^{i}_{(r)}(Y)
0
0
\cdots
.

So
H^{i}_{(r)}(Y)=0=H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(Y)
, but this just means
Y\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
.
Now consider
X\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
for the stability under the suspension functor. By definition of the stable category and its triangulated structure we have a triangle of the form

X
\mathrm{P}_{N}(X)
\Sigma(X)
\Sigma(X).
\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}}

We get again by Corollary
7.8
long exact sequences

\cdots
H^{i-N}_{(r)}(\Sigma(X))
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(X)
0
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(\Sigma(X))
H^{i}_{(r)}(X)
0
H^{i}_{(r)}(\Sigma(X))
H^{i+r}_{(r)}(X)
\cdots
.

So we have for all
1\leq r\leq N-1
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}

H^{i}_{(r)}(X)\cong H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(\Sigma(X)).

This proves the claim for
\Sigma(X)
. For
\Sigma^{-1}(X)
consider the defining triangle of
\Sigma^{-1}(X)
:

\Sigma^{-1}(X)
\mathrm{I}_{N}(X)
X
X.
\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{I}}

This gives again long exact sequences:

\cdots
H^{i-N}_{(r)}(X)
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(\Sigma^{-1}(X))
0
H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(X)
H^{i}_{(r)}(\Sigma^{-1}(X))
0
H^{i}_{(r)}(X)
H^{i+r}_{(r)}(\Sigma^{-1}(X))
\cdots
.

So we get for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq r\leq N-1
that

H^{i}_{(r)}(\Sigma^{-1}(X))\cong H^{i-N+r}_{(N-r)}(X).

which finished the claim. ∎

Now we will finally define the derived category in the way of
[
12
]
.

Definition 7.12
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the
derived category of
N
-complexes in
\mathcal{A}
is defined as the Verdier-quotient

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathcal{A}):=\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})/\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}.

The following Lemma for
N
-complexes in a ring can be found in
[
13
]
as Proposition 1.5, and allows to calculate acyclicity of an
N
-complex using just one fixed
r
with
1\leq r\leq N-1
. Although this is a quite powerful statement the proof does not give a lot insight, so we omit it.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
R
be a ring and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have for
X\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
that
H_{(r)}^{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq r\leq N-1
if and only if there is one
1\leq r\leq N-1
such that
H_{(r)}^{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Proposition 1.5 in
[
13
]
.
∎

We will later need the following statement in order to control acyclicity on the level of morphisms.

Proposition 7.14
.

Let
R
be a ring and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have that
X\in K_{N}^{\emptyset}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
if and only if for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(\mu_{1}^{i}(R),X)=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.13
it suffices to show that
H_{(1)}^{j}(X)=0
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
if and only if
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(\mu_{1}^{i}(R),X)=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Hence consider First
x\in\ker(d_{X})^{i}\subset X^{i}
. We want to show that
x\in\mathrm{im}(d^{N-1})
.

This
x
defines a map

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{i}(R)

\displaystyle\to X^{i}

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\mapsto x

which by assumption has to factor as
\mu_{1}^{i}(R)\to\mathrm{I}_{N}(\mu_{1}^{i}(R))\xrightarrow{\varphi}X
in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
. But we have
\mathrm{I}_{N}(\mu_{1}^{i}(R))=\mu_{N}^{i}(R)
. So define
y\in X^{i-N+1}
by

y:=\varphi^{i-N+1}(1).

This now yields

d_{X}^{N-1}(y)=d_{X}^{N-1}\circ\varphi^{i-N+1}(1)=\varphi^{i}\circ d_{\mu_{N}^{i}(R)}^{N-1}(1)=\varphi^{i}(1)=x,

so
d^{N-1}(y)=x
. Since this now implies that
x\in\mathrm{im}(d^{N-1})
we have

H_{(1)}^{i}(X)=\ker(d)/\mathrm{im}(d^{N-1})=0

as desired.

For the other implication let
\varphi:\mu_{1}^{i}(R)\to X
. This morphism is uniquely defined by
x:=\varphi^{i}(1)
. This is an element of
\ker(d_{X})
, since
d\circ\varphi=\varphi\circ d=0
. The acyclicity of
X
now means that there exists a
y\in X^{i-N+1}
such that
d^{N-1}(y)=x
. Now consider the morphism
\psi:\mu_{N}^{i}(R)\to X
defined by
\psi^{i-N+1}(1)=y
. This
\psi
has the property
\psi^{i}(1)=x
and hence
\varphi
factors through
\mu_{N}^{i}(R)
. But this just means that
\varphi=0
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
.
∎

7.2.
The category of h-projectives

Similar to the case of ordinary chain complexes we will establish the notion of h-projective
N
-complexes. This allows to realize the derived category of
N
-complexes of a ring already as subcategory of the homotopy category. Furthermore it advantageously avoids roof diagrams for calculations of morphisms. However, due to our general setting, these two approaches might not coincide. Nonetheless we will show in the next subsection that this works out for
N
-complexes of modules over ring.

Definition 7.15
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. An object
X\in\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is called
h-projective
if for all acyclic objects
Y
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
the abelian group
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,Y)
is trivial. We refer to the full subcategory of h-projective objects in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
as
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.

Since the derived category has naturally the structure of a triangulated category, this should hold as well for the category of h-projectives.

Proposition 7.16
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the category
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is a thick triangulated subcategory of
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
which is closed under coproducts if
\mathcal{A}
is closed under coproducts.

Proof.

We again check the properties separately:

triangulated:

First consider a triangle

X
Y
Z
\Sigma(X)
f
g
h

in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
such that
X
and
Z
are h-projective. Then we get for all
T\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
a long exact sequence since
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is a triangulated category

\cdots
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(\Sigma X,T)
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(Z,T)
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(Y,T)
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,T)
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(\Sigma^{-1}Z,T)
\cdots
.
\circ g
\circ f

Hence we get for all acyclic
T
an exact sequence of the form

0
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(Y,T)
0

and so
Y
has to be h-projective as well.

To show the stability under suspension, just observe that we have

\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(\Sigma(X),T)\cong\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,\Sigma^{-1}(T))\;\text{ and }\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(\Sigma^{-1}(X),T)\cong\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,\Sigma(T))

by the definition of a triangulated category for all
T\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
.
But this now shows the claim since
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
is triangulated.

thick:

Let
X
be a direct summand of
Y
, where
Y
is an h-projective
N
-complex. Then we have
X\oplus Z\cong Y
for some
Z\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Now since
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(\_,T)
is an additive functor, we get:

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X\oplus Z,T)=\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,T)\oplus\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(Z,T).

But again specialization to
T
acyclic yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,T)\oplus\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(Z,T)=\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X\oplus Z,T)=0

and so
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,T)=0
.

coproducts:

Assume that
\mathcal{A}
admits coproducts. Then by Proposition
1.10
and Lemma
6.2
the category
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
admits coproducts. Now the claim follows since for
T\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
:

\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(\bigoplus_{i\in I}X_{i},T)=\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{K}_{N}(X_{i},T)=\prod_{i\in I}0=0.

So all claimed properties hold.
∎

Furthermore the derived category has the property that any acyclic object in it is zero, which should hold as well for h-projectives.

Proposition 7.17
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
X\in\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then
X
is acyclic if and only if it is isomorphic to zero in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

If
X\cong 0
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
, then
H_{(r)}^{i}(X)=H_{(r)}^{i}(0)=0
and hence it is acyclic. For the converse observe that, since
X
is both acyclic and h-projective, we have
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,X)=0
, now consider
\mathrm{Id}_{X}\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,X)=0
. So we get
\mathrm{Id}_{X}=0
and so
X\cong 0
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.
∎

Now finally the last property that makes the h-projectives resemble the derived category we can prove in general is that a morphism is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
if and only if it is an
N
-quasi-isomorphism.

Proposition 7.18
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
f:X\to Y
a morphism in
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then
f
is an
N
-quasi-isomorphism if and only if
f
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

“
\Leftarrow
”:

This implication is just Corollary
7.6
.

“
\Rightarrow
”:

Let
f:X\to Y
be an
N
-quasi-isomorphism between h-projective objects. Then we get by Corollary
7.9
a triangle

X
Y
\mathrm{Cone}(f)
\Sigma(X)
f
g
h

in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
such that
H_{(r)}^{i}(\mathrm{Cone}(f))=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq r\leq N-1
. Hence
\mathrm{Cone}(f)
is acyclic. On the other hand
\mathrm{Cone}(f)
has to be h-projective by Proposition
7.16
. But this means by Proposition
7.17
that
\mathrm{Cone}(f)\cong 0
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
, and so
f
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. ∎

The following proposition will allow us to use certain h-projectives as building blocks for our semifree resolutions.

Proposition 7.19
.

Let
R
be a ring and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then every
N
-complex in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
which is levelwise free with vanishing differential is
h
-projective.

Proof.

Let
X
be such an
N
-complex and
T
an acyclic
N
-complex. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(X,T)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(\mu_{1}^{i}(R^{\oplus I_{i}}),T))

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\prod_{I_{i}}\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(\mu_{1}^{i}(R),T)=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\prod_{I_{i}}0=0.

Here the second to last equation holds by Proposition
7.14
.
∎

Although we do not have a natural equivalence
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})\cong\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
in general, we still get a fully faithful functor in general. To show this we will need the following general property of triangulated categories.

Proposition 7.20
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulated category and

X
Y
Z
\Sigma(X)
f
g
h

a triangle in
\mathcal{T}
. Then
h=0
if and only if
g
admits a splitting
s:Z\to Y
.

Proof.

First let
h=0
. Then the following diagram commutes and hence the dashed arrow exists:

X
Y
Z
\Sigma(X)
.
X
X\oplus Z
Z
\Sigma(X)
f
g
0
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}\\
0\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathrm{Id}\end{bmatrix}
0
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\widehat{s}

The commutativity of the middle square now yields

g\circ\widehat{s}\circ\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\mathrm{Id}_{Z}\end{bmatrix}=\mathrm{Id}_{Z}.

So we get the split
s:=\widehat{s}\circ\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\mathrm{Id}_{Z}\end{bmatrix}:Z\to Y
.

For the other direction just observe that if he have a split
s:Z\to Y
the diagram

X
Y
Z
\Sigma(X)
.
X
X\oplus Z
Z
\Sigma(X)
,
f
g
h
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}\\
0\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathrm{Id}\end{bmatrix}
0
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\begin{bmatrix}f&s\end{bmatrix}

commutes. So

h=h\circ\mathrm{Id}=\mathrm{Id}\circ 0=0

as claimed. ∎

Remark 7.21
.

We will use in the following proof that morphisms in the derived category, or any Verdier quotient can be characterized as roof diagrams or coroof diagrams. We refer the reader for the notions of these to the book by Neeman
[
20
]
. In particular we refer to Definition 2.1.11, Lemma 2.1.14 and Remark 2.1.25.

Theorem 7.22
.

The functor
\pi:\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
induces a bijection

\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,Y)\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathrm{D}_{N}({\mathcal{A}})(X,Y)\text{ for }X\in\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A}).

In particular the restriction of
\pi
to
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is fully faithful.

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,Y)
, where
X\in\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Then we have that
f
is described by a roof diagram of the form
(s,g)
for
s:W\to X
an
N
-quasi-isomorphism and
g:W\to Y
by the construction of
\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
as Verdier quotient. Now consider the diagram

X
Y
C(s)
\Sigma^{-1}(C(s))
W
s
\varphi
\psi
g

in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
, where
C(s)
is the cone of
s
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Since
s
is an
N
-quasi-isomorphism we get by Corollary
7.9
that
C(s)
is acyclic, but this yields
\varphi=0
since
X\in\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Using that
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is triangulated and Proposition
7.20
we get a splitting
t:X\to W
of
s
and the following diagram commutes

\hbox to74.6pt{\vbox to64.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 16.0327pt\lower-4.18877pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
