\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Hence
f
can be represented by the equivalent roof
(\mathrm{Id},g\circ t)
, so
f=\pi(g\circ t)
which proves that
\pi
induces a surjection.

To show that it is also injective consider a morphism
g:X\to Y
such that
\pi(g)=0
. The morphism
\pi(g)
is now described by the coroof diagram
(g,\mathrm{Id}_{Y})
which is by assumption equivalent to
(0,\mathrm{Id}_{Y})
hence we have an
N
-complex
Z
and an
N
-quasi-isomorphisms
\varphi:Y\to Z
such that the following diagram commutes in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
:

X
Y
Y
Y.
Z
g
\mathrm{Id}_{Y}
0
\mathrm{Id}_{Y}
0
\varphi
\varphi
\varphi

In particular we get
\varphi\circ g=0
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Now since
\varphi
is an
N
-quasi-isomorphism we get the following triangle in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})

Y
Z
\mathrm{Cone}_{\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})}(\varphi)
\Sigma Y
\varphi

where
\mathrm{Cone}_{\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})}(\varphi)
is acyclic. Since
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
is triangulated this induces a long exact sequence of the form

\cdots
\mathrm{K}_{N}(X,\Sigma^{-1}C)
\mathrm{K}_{N}(X,Y)
\mathrm{K}_{N}(X,Z)
\mathrm{K}_{N}(X,C)
\cdots
\varphi\circ

where we denote by
C
the cone of
\varphi
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Since
X
is h-projective we get

\mathrm{K}_{N}(X,C)\cong 0\cong\mathrm{K}_{N}(X,\Sigma^{-1}C),

as
C
is acyclic by Proposition
7.1
and
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})^{\emptyset}
is triangulated by Proposition
7.11
. But this means that
\varphi\circ:\mathrm{K}_{N}(X,Y)\to\mathrm{K}_{N}(X,Z)
is an isomorphism. So since
\varphi\circ g=0
we get that
g
already has to vanish in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(X,Y)
. This shows that
\pi
is injective on
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,Y)
.

To conclude the in particular part just observe that
\pi
induces by the above proof a bijection
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,Y)\to\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathcal{A})(X,Y)
for all
X,Y\in\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
.
∎

7.3.
Semifree resolutions

To conclude the section we will show that the canonical functor
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
restricts to an equivalence
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
if we have
\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
for a ring
R
. By Theorem
7.22
we only have to show that this functor is essentially surjective. To do this we will give here a construction following the idea presented in the remark after Lemma 13.3 in “Dg-quotients of Dg-categories” by Drinfeld
[
5
]
.

The recipe for this construction is to consider a certain type of objects, namely semifree modules, and show that every complex admits a semifree replacement in
\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Since these are h-projective this will give essential surjectivity of the projection functor.

So let us first define what it means for an
N
-complex to be semifree and why these
N
-complexes are h-projective.

Definition 7.23
.

Let
R
be a ring and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. We call an
N
-complex
F
in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}

semifree
if there exists a filtration
0=F_{0}\subset F_{1}\subset F_{2}\subset F_{3}\subset...
, such that:

(1)

F=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{n},

(2)

F_{i}/F_{i-1}
is levelwise free with vanishing differential
\forall i\geq 1.

Proposition 7.24
.

Let
R
be a ring,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
M
a semifree
N
-complex in
R
. Then we have
M\in\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
.

Proof.

Since
M
is semifree we have a filtration
0=F_{0}\subset F_{1}\subset F_{2}\subset F_{3}...
such that
M=\mathrm{colim}_{i}F_{i}
. Now we have by Proposition
7.19
that
F_{0}
and
F_{i}/F_{i-1}
are h-projective and levelwise free. Assume that
F_{i-1}
is h-projective, then we have a short exact sequence of the form

0
F_{i-1}
F_{i}
F_{i}/F_{i-1}
0

which levelwise splits, since
F_{i}/F_{i-1}
is levelwise free. So the sequence induces a triangle

F_{i-1}
F_{i}
F_{i}/F_{i-1}
\Sigma F_{i-1}

in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
by Corollary
5.11
. Now since
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
is a triangulated subcategory and both
F_{i-1}
and
F_{i}/F_{i-1}
are h-projective, we get that
F_{i}
is h-projective. So inductively all
F_{j}
are h-projective and all inclusions
\varphi_{i}:F_{i}\hookrightarrow F_{i+1}
levelwise split.
To show now that finally
M
is h-projective consider the short exact sequence

0
\bigoplus\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}F_{i}
\bigoplus\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}F_{i}
M
0
\Psi

exhibiting
M
as
\mathrm{colim}_{i}F_{i}
. Here
\Psi
is the morphism induced by the composition

F_{i}\xrightarrow{\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{F_{i}}\\
-\varphi_{i}\end{bmatrix}}F_{i}\oplus F_{i+1}\xhookrightarrow{\begin{bmatrix}\iota_{i}&\iota_{i+1}\end{bmatrix}}\bigoplus\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}F_{i},

where
\varphi_{i}
is the inclusion
F_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{i+1}
and
\iota_{j}
is the inclusion
F_{j}\hookrightarrow\bigoplus\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}F_{i}
. Now observe that
\Psi
is of the form

\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{F_{0}}&0&0&0&\cdots\\
-\varphi_{0}&\mathrm{Id}_{F_{1}}&0&0&\cdots\\
0&-\varphi_{1}&\mathrm{Id}_{F_{2}}&0&\cdots\\
0&0&-\varphi_{2}&\mathrm{Id}_{F_{3}}&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}.

Consider the following morphism in
\mathrm{Gr}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
, the category of graded modules over
R
:

\begin{bmatrix}0&-\psi_{0}&-\psi_{0}\circ\psi_{1}&-\psi_{0}\circ\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2}&\cdots\\
0&0&\psi_{1}&\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2}&\cdots\\
0&0&0&\psi_{2}&\cdots\\
0&0&0&0&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{bmatrix},

where
\psi_{i}
denotes a split of
\varphi_{i}
in
\mathrm{Gr}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
, which exists by the argument above. This is a levelwise split of
\Psi
, since one can calculate

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0&-\psi_{0}&-\psi_{0}\circ\psi_{1}&-\psi_{0}\circ\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2}&\cdots\\
0&0&-\psi_{1}&-\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2}&\cdots\\
0&0&0&-\psi_{2}&\cdots\\
0&0&0&0&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{F_{0}}&0&0&0&\cdots\\
-\varphi_{0}&\mathrm{Id}_{F_{1}}&0&0&\cdots\\
0&-\varphi_{1}&\mathrm{Id}_{F_{2}}&0&\cdots\\
0&0&-\varphi_{2}&\mathrm{Id}_{F_{3}}&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{F_{0}}&0&0&0&\cdots\\
0&\mathrm{Id}_{F_{1}}&0&0&\cdots\\
0&0&\mathrm{Id}_{F_{2}}&0&\cdots\\
0&0&0&\mathrm{Id}_{F_{3}}&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}

where the zeroes above the diagonal appear since the entries there are

-\psi_{i}\circ...\circ\psi_{j}+\psi_{i}\circ...\circ\psi_{j}\circ\psi_{j+1}\circ\varphi_{j+1}=-\psi_{i}\circ...\circ\psi_{j}+\psi_{i}\circ...\circ\psi_{j}=0.

Hence the sequence levelwise splits. This gives again that the sequence induces a triangle

\bigoplus\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}F_{i}
\bigoplus\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}F_{i}
M
\Sigma\bigoplus\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}F_{i}

in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathcal{A})
. Now since
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
is by Proposition
7.16
closed under coproducts we have that
\bigoplus\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}F_{i}
is h-projective and again since
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
is triangulated we get that
M
is h-projective as claimed.
∎

Having established this, let us construct our semifree resolutions:

Theorem 7.25
.

Let
R
be a ring,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
M
an
N
-complex in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. Then there exists a semifree
N
-complex
F
and a morphism
f:F\to M
such that
f
is an
N
-quasi-isomorphism. Furthermore,
f
can be chosen surjective and such that the induced maps
\ker(d^{r}_{F})\to\ker(d^{r}_{M})
are surjective for all
1\leq r\leq N-1
.

Proof.

Let the
N
-complex
F_{1}
be defined by
F_{1}^{j}:=\langle\ker(d_{M}\mid_{M^{j}})\rangle_{R}
and by setting
d_{F_{1}}=0
, let
f_{1}:F_{1}\to M
be the morphism defined levelwise by the inclusions
\iota:\ker(d_{M}\mid_{M^{j}})\hookrightarrow M^{j}
. This defines a morphism of
N
-complexes since
f_{1}\circ d_{F_{1}}=0=d_{M}\circ f_{1}
.

Now assume
F_{i-1}
and
f_{i-1}
are already constructed and let
B_{i-1}
be the graded set defined levelwise by

B_{i-1}^{j}:=\{(b,y_{b})\in\ker(d^{N-1}_{F_{i-1}}\mid_{F_{i-1}^{j+1}})\times M^{j}\mid f_{i-1}(b)=d(y_{b})\}.

Then define

\displaystyle F_{i}^{j}

\displaystyle:=F_{i-1}^{j}\oplus\langle B_{i-1}^{j}\rangle_{R},

\displaystyle d_{F_{i}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}d_{F_{i-1}}&d_{B_{i-1}}\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}

where
d_{B_{i-1}}((b,y_{b})):=b
.
This makes
F_{i}
into an
N
-complex, since
d_{F_{i-1}}^{N}=0
by assumption and

\displaystyle d^{N}_{F_{i}}((b,y_{b}))

\displaystyle=d_{F_{i-1}}^{N-1}(b)=0,

since by definition
b\in\ker(d^{N-1}_{F_{i-1}}\mid_{F_{i-1}^{j+1}})
.
Furthermore define

\displaystyle f_{i}:F_{i}

\displaystyle\to M,

\displaystyle(x,r(b,y_{b}))

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{i-1}(x)+ry_{b}.

This defines a morphism of
N
-complexes since:

\displaystyle d\circ f_{i}(x,r(b,y_{b}))=

\displaystyle d(f_{i-1}(x)+ry_{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{i-1}(d(x))+rd(y_{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{i-1}(d(x))+rf_{i-1}(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{i-1}(d(x)+rb)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{i-1}\circ d_{F_{i}}(x,r(b,y_{b})).

Now define

F:=\lim_{\longrightarrow}(0=F_{0}\hookrightarrow F_{1}\hookrightarrow F_{2}\hookrightarrow F_{3}\hookrightarrow...).

We get a natural map by the definition of the colimit which will be the desired
f:F\to M
.

F_{0}=0
F_{1}
F_{2}
F_{3}
F_{4}
\cdot\cdot\cdot
\rightsquigarrow
M
F
M
f_{0}=0
f_{1}
f_{2}
f_{3}
f_{4}
f

The just defined
F
is semifree since it is defined as the colimit of inclusions, so we have a filtration
0=F_{0}\subset F_{1}\subset F_{2}\subset F_{3}\subset F_{4}...
and
F=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{i}
. Furthermore we have that

(F_{i}/F_{i-1})^{j}=\langle B_{i-1}^{j}\rangle_{R},

and hence
F_{i}/F_{i-1}
is levelwise free, and the differential vanishes since by definition
d_{F_{i}}(x)\in F_{i-1}
. So we have that
F
is semifree.

Now it just remains to show that
f:F\to M
is a surjective
N
-quasi-isomorphism which is surjective on
\ker(d^{r}_{M})
for all
1\leq r\leq N-1
:

Consider
f|_{\ker(d^{r}_{F})}:\ker(d^{r}_{F})\to\ker(d^{r}_{M})
for
1\leq r\leq N
:

Let
y\in\ker(d_{M}^{r})
and define
y_{i}:=d^{r-i}(y)\in\ker(d^{i})
, in particular
y_{1}\in\ker(d)
, hence there exists a
x_{1}:=1y_{1}\in\ker(d_{F_{1}})
such that
f(x_{1})=y_{1}
. So we get
(x_{1},y_{2})\in B_{1}
. By construction this implies:

f_{2}(0,1(x_{1},y_{2}))=y_{2}.

So let
x_{2}:=(0,1(x_{1},y_{2}))\in\ker(d^{2}_{F_{2}})
.

Assume now that
x_{i}\in\ker(d^{i}_{F_{i}})
for some
1\leq i\leq r-1
has been constructed such that
f_{i}(x_{i})=y_{i}
, then
(x_{i},y_{i+1})\in B_{i}
and one gets

f_{i+1}(0,1(x_{i},y_{i+1}))=y_{i+1}.

So define
x_{i+1}:=(0,1(x_{i},y_{i+1}))\in\ker(d^{i+1}_{F_{i}+1})
.

Now one gets inductively
x_{r}\in\ker(d^{r}_{F_{r}})
with
f_{r}(x_{r})=y_{r}=y
and hence the induced map
f_{r}:\ker(d^{r}_{F_{r}})\to\ker(d^{r}_{M})
is surjective. This shows that
f|_{\ker(d^{r}_{F})}:\ker(d^{r}_{F})\to\ker(d^{r}_{M})
is surjective. In particular
f
is surjective since
\ker(d_{M}^{N})=M
.

To prove that
f
is an
N
-quasi-isomorphism it remains to show that
f
is injective on cohomology.

Let
x\in\ker(d^{r}_{F})
with
y:=f(x)\in\mathrm{im}(d^{N-r}_{M})\subset\ker(d_{M}^{r})
. Then there exists an
F_{j}
such that
x\in F_{j}
.
Now, since by assumption
f(x)\in\mathrm{im}(d^{N-r}_{M})
, there exists a
y_{N-r}\in M
such that
d^{N-r}(y_{N-r})=y
and so
(x,d^{N-r-1}y_{N-r})\in B_{j}
and

f_{j+1}(0\oplus 1(x,d^{N-r-1}y_{N-r}))=d^{N-r-1}y_{N-r}.

Define
x_{1}:=0\oplus 1(x,d^{N-r-1}y_{N-r})
.

Assume now that
x_{i}
has been constructed for
1\leq i\leq N-r-1
such that
f(x_{i})=d^{N-r-i}(y_{N-r})
. Then
(x_{i},d^{N-r-i-1}y_{N-r})\in B_{j-i}
and

f_{j+i+1}(0\oplus 1(x_{i},d^{N-r-(i+1)}y_{N-r}))=d^{N-r-(i+1)}y_{N-r}.

Hence define
x_{i+1}:=0\oplus 1(x_{i},d^{N-r-(i+1)}y_{N-r})
.

So one gets inductively
x_{N-r}
. Now we compute by the definition of
F
:

\displaystyle d_{F}^{N-r}(x_{N-r})=

\displaystyle d_{F}^{N-r}(x_{N-r-1},y_{N-r})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{F}^{N-r-1}(x_{N-r-1})=d_{F}^{N-r-1}(x_{N-r-2},d_{M}y_{N-r})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{F}^{N-r-2}(x_{N-r-2})=d_{F}^{N-r-2}(x_{N-r-3},d_{M}^{2}y_{N-r})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{F}(x_{1})=d_{F}(x,d_{M}^{N-r}y_{N-r})=x.

Hence
x\in\mathrm{im}(d^{N-r}_{F})
. This now shows that
f
is injective in cohomology which concludes the proof.
∎

Finally using this we can show that
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
indeed is a model for the derived category of
N
-complexes over a ring
R
.

Corollary 7.26
.

Let
R
be a ring and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have that the canonical functor
\pi:\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\to\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
restricts to an equivalence of categories:

\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}).

Proof.

We know by Theorem
7.22
that
\pi\mid_{\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})}
is fully faithful, hence it just remains to show that it is essentially surjective. But by Theorem
7.25
we have that every
X\in\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
is
N
-quasi-isomorphic to a semifree
N
-complex
F_{X}
, which is by Proposition
7.24
h-projective. So
\pi(X)\cong\pi(F_{X})
, which implies essential surjectivity.
∎

8.
A model structure on
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})

We will establish a model structure on the category of
N
-complexes over a ring
R
, which will give as model theoretic homotopy category the derived category of
N
-complexes over
R
. In particular this shows that the derived category of
N
-complexes over a ring is indeed the Gabriel-Zismann localisation of
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
along the
N
-quasi-isomorphisms. To show this, we will always consider a ring and the category of
N
-complexes over it. So we fix throughout this section a ring
R
and a natural number
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

The same model structure was constructed by J. Gillespie and M. Hovey in
[
8
]
. However our construction is independent and more hands on, since J. Gillespie and M. Hovey used the theory of graded n-Gorenstein K-algebras whereas our proof is direct. Furthermore it is no surprise that both constructions yield this model structure, since it is the obvious generalization of the projective model structure on the category of chain complexes.

The first thing we will need are the following collections of morphisms, since we will use the small object argument to cofibrantly generate a model structure. To do so we will use Theorem 2.1.19 in
[
11
]
.

Definition 8.1
.

Define the following classes of morphisms in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
:

(1)

J:=\left\{\iota_{i}:0\to\mu_{N}^{i}(R)\middle|i\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}
.

(2)

I:=J\cup\left\{\iota_{i,r}:\mu_{r}^{i}(R)\to\mu_{N}^{i}(R)\middle|i\in\mathbb{Z}\ 0<r<N\right\}
, where
\iota_{i,r}^{k}=\mathrm{Id}_{R}
whenever possible and zero otherwise.

(3)

W:=\{\text{N-quasi-isomorphisms}\}\subset\mathrm{Mor}(\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}))
.

The set
I
will become the set of generating cofibrations and
J
the set of generating trivial cofibrations. However in order to classify the induced fibrations, trivial fibrations and trivial cofibrations we will need the notion of left and right lifting property respectively.

Definition 8.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
\Psi\subset\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
a class of morphisms. Then we say that:

(1)

A morphism
\varphi:X\to Y
has the
left lifting property with respect to
\Psi
if for all
(\psi:M\to N)\in\Psi
and all commutative squares of the form

N
M
Y
X
\varphi
\psi

there exists a lift
h:Y\to M
such that the following diagram commutes:

N.
M
Y
X
\varphi
h
\psi

(2)

A morphism
\varphi:X\to Y
has the
right lifting property with respect to
\Psi
if for all
(\psi:M\to N)\in\Psi
and all commutative squares of the form

N
M
Y
X
\varphi
\psi

there exists a lift
h:N\to X
such that the following diagram commutes:

N
M
Y.
X
\varphi
h
\psi

(3)

We denote the collections of morphisms with the left and right lifting property with respect to
\Psi
by
\boxslash\Psi
and
\Psi\boxslash
respectively.

Remark 8.3
.

The morphisms
\iota_{i,N-1}
are of the form:

\cdots
0
0
R
\cdots
R
R
0
\cdots
\cdots
0
R
R
\cdots
R
R
0
\cdots
.
\cdots
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}
\mathrm{Id}

Remark 8.4
.

Since we will be working with lifting problems of morphisms with source objects of the form
\mu_{j}^{i}(R)
, we will take the shorthand notation
1^{k}
for the element
1\in R=\mu_{j}^{i}(R)^{k}
for
i-j+1\leq k\leq i
.

Now we can prove statements which are analogous to the case of the projective model structure for ordinary chain complexes. This will give us a classification of the class
J\boxslash
.

Proposition 8.5
.

The class
J\boxslash
is the class of levelwise epimorphisms.

Proof.

We will prove that both classes mutually include each other:

“
\subseteq
”:

Let
p:X\to Y
be in
J\boxslash
. So
p
has the right lifting property with respect to all morphisms in
J
. Furthermore let
y\in Y^{i}
. To show that there exists a preimage of
y
under
p
consider the following lifting problem:

Y,
X
\mu_{N}^{i+N-1}(R)
0
p
\varphi_{y}

here
\varphi_{y}
is the map uniquely defined by
1^{i}\mapsto y
, this is well defined by the adjunction between
\mu_{N}^{i+N-1}(\_)
and
(\_)^{i}
from Proposition
1.8
. Now there exists, since
p\in J\boxslash
, a lift
h:\mu_{N}^{i+N-1}(R)\to X
, such that the following diagram commutes:

Y.
X
\mu_{N}^{i+N-1}(R)
0
p
h
\varphi_{y}

Now
p\circ h=\varphi_{y}
. This means if we define
x:=h(1^{i})
, that
y=\varphi_{y}(1^{i})=p(h(1^{i}))=p(x)
and hence we have a preimage of
y
, so
p
is surjective.

“
\supseteq
”:

Let
p:X\to Y
be a levelwise epimorphism. Consider the following lifting problem:

Y.
X
\mu_{N}^{i+N-1}(R)
0
p
\varphi

Now by the adjunction
\mu_{N}^{i+N-1}(\_)\dashv(\_)^{i}
we get that the morphism
\varphi
comes from a map
\psi:R\to Y^{i}
, which is uniquely determined by the image of
1\in R
, let
y:=\psi(1)
. Now, since
p
is a levelwise epimorphism, there exists
x\in X^{i}
such that
p(x)=y
. Consider
h^{\prime}:R\to X^{i}
, defined by
h^{\prime}(1)=x
. Now we get an adjoint map
h
to
h^{\prime}
, and so the following diagram commutes:

Y.
X
\mu_{N}^{i+N-1}(R)
0
p
\varphi
h

Since
i
and
\varphi
were arbitrary, we get
p\in J\boxslash
.

∎

Proposition 8.6
.

The class
I\boxslash
is precisely the class of levelwise surjective
N
-quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

We will prove this by showing that each class is included in the other:

“
\subseteq
”:

Let
(p:X\to Y)\in I\boxslash
. First observe that
J\subset I
directly gives
I\boxslash\subset J\boxslash
. So by Proposition
8.5
the morphism
p
has to be a levelwise epimorphism.

Hence it remains to show that
p
is an
N
-quasi-isomorphism. First we show that
p
induces an epimorphism on cohomology. For this consider
y\in\ker(d_{Y}^{r})^{i-N+1}
and the lifting problem:

Y,
X
\mu_{N}^{i}(R)
\mu_{N-r}^{i}(R)
p
0
\iota_{i,N-r}
\varphi_{y}

where the bottom map is the map induced by
1^{i-N+1}\mapsto y
. This diagram commutes since
y\in\ker(d_{Y}^{r})
. Since
p\in I\boxslash
there is a lift
h:\mu_{N}^{i}(R)\to X
such that the following square commutes:

Y.
X
\mu_{N}^{i}(R)
\mu_{N-r}^{i}(R)
p
0
\iota_{i,N-r}
\varphi_{y}
h

But the commutativity of the top triangle just means that
x:=h(1^{i-N+1})\in\ker(d^{r})
and the bottom triangle gives
p(x)=y
. So
p
induces a surjection on
\ker(d^{r})
for all
1\leq r\leq N-1
and hence
H_{(r)}(p)
is an epimorphism as well.

In order to show that
H_{(r)}(p)
is an monomorphism it suffices to show, that if for an
x\in\ker(d_{X}^{r})^{i-r+1}
there exists an
y^{\prime}
such that
y:=p(x)=d_{Y}^{N-r}(y^{\prime})
, then there exists an
x^{\prime}\in X
such that
d_{X}^{N-r}(x^{\prime})=x
.

To prove this, consider the lifting problem:

Y,
X
\mu_{N}^{i}(R)
\mu_{r}^{i}(R)
p
\varphi_{x}
\iota_{i,r}
\varphi_{y^{\prime}}

where
\varphi_{y^{\prime}}
is again defined by
\varphi_{y^{\prime}}(1^{i-N+1})=y^{\prime}
and
\varphi_{x}
is defined by
\varphi_{x}(1^{i-r+1})=x
. Hence the diagram indeed commutes, since
p(x)=d_{Y}^{N-r}(y^{\prime})
. Now we again get a morphism
h:\mu_{N}^{i}(R)\to X
such that the square

Y.
X
\mu_{N}^{i}(R)
\mu_{r}^{i}(R)
p
\varphi_{x}
\iota_{i,r}
\varphi_{y^{\prime}}
h

commutes. The commutativity of the top triangle now yields that

\displaystyle d_{X}^{N-r}\circ h(1^{i-N+1})=h\circ d_{\mu_{N}^{i}(R)}^{N-r}(1^{i-N+1})=h(1^{i-r+1})=h\circ\iota_{i,r}(1^{i-r+1})=\varphi_{x}(1^{i-r+1})=x.

Hence for
x^{\prime}:=h(1^{i-N+1})
we get
d^{N-r}(x^{\prime})=x
, and we have that
H_{(r)}(p)
is injective. Which proves the claim.

“
\supseteq
”:

Let
p:X\to Y
be a surjective
N
-quasi-isomorphism and consider a lifting problem:

Y.
X
\mu_{N}^{i}(R)
\mu_{N-r}^{i}(R)
p
\psi
\iota_{i,N-r}
\varphi

Now since
p
is surjective there is a short exact sequence in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
of the form

\hbox to126.2pt{\vbox to21.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 67.6327pt\lower-3.21124pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Hence we can interpret
\ker(p)
as a subcomplex of
X
, and since
p
is an
N
-quasi-isomorphism we have that
H_{(r)}(\ker(p))=0
by Lemma
7.7
. Let
x^{\prime}\in X^{i-N+1}
be a preimage of
y:=\varphi(1^{i-N+1})
under
p
and let
\widehat{h}:\mu_{N}^{i}(R)\to X
be the map defined by
\widehat{h}(1^{i-N+1})=x^{\prime}
. Now this morphism makes the bottom triangle in the following diagram commute,

Y,
X
\mu_{N}^{i}(R)
\mu_{N-r}^{i}(R)
↯
p
\psi
\iota_{i,N-r}
\varphi
\widehat{h}

but in general not the top triangle. To solve this problem, let
x:=\psi(1^{i-N+r+1})
. now consider
d^{r}_{X}(x^{\prime})-x
. This is an element of
\ker(p)
, since the commutativity of the outer square yields

\displaystyle p(d^{r}_{X}(x^{\prime})-x)

\displaystyle=d^{r}_{Y}(p(x^{\prime}))-p(x)

\displaystyle=d^{r}_{Y}(y)-p(\psi(1^{i-N+r+1}))

\displaystyle=d^{r}_{Y}(y)-\varphi(\iota_{i,r}(1^{i-N+r+1}))

\displaystyle=d^{r}_{Y}(y)-\varphi(1^{i-N+r+1})

\displaystyle=d^{r}_{Y}(y)-\varphi(d^{r}(1^{i-N+1}))

\displaystyle=d^{r}_{Y}(y)-d^{r}(\varphi(1^{i-N+1}))

\displaystyle=d^{r}_{Y}(y)-d_{Y}^{r}(y)=0.

Furthermore we have
d^{r}(x^{\prime}),x\in\ker(d^{N-r})
, since on one hand
d_{\ker(p)}^{N}=0
and on the other hand
x=\psi(1^{i-N+r+1})
and
\mu^{i}_{N-r}(R)\in\mathrm{C}_{N-r}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
.

So
d^{r}_{X}(x^{\prime})-x=0
in
H_{(N-r)}(\ker(p))
. Therefore there exists an element
\widehat{x}\in\ker(p)
such that
d^{r}(\widehat{x})=d^{r}_{X}(x^{\prime})-x
. Now we may replace
\widehat{h}
by the unique map
h:\mu_{N}^{i}(R)\to X
which satisfies
h(1^{i-N+1})=x^{\prime}-\widehat{x}
. Then the diagram

Y
X
\mu_{N}^{i}(R)
\mu_{N-r}^{i}(R)
p
\psi
\iota_{i,N-r}
\varphi
h

indeed commutes since

\displaystyle h\circ\iota_{i,N-r}(1^{i-N+r+1})

\displaystyle=h(1^{i-N+r+1})=d^{r}_{X}\circ h(1^{i-N+1})

\displaystyle=d^{r}_{X}(x^{\prime}-\widehat{x})=d^{r}_{X}(x^{\prime})-d^{r}_{X}(x^{\prime})+x=x=\psi(1^{i-N+r+1})

and

\displaystyle p\circ h(1^{i-N+1})=p(x^{\prime}-\widehat{x})=p(x^{\prime})-0=y=\varphi(1^{i-N+1}).

Hence the lifting problem has a solution. Now
p\in J\boxslash
remains to be shown, but this is clear by Proposition
8.5
. So we altogether get
p\in I\boxslash
.

∎

Proposition 8.7
.

The class
\boxslash(J\boxslash)
is precisely the class of split monomorphisms with projective cokernel. In particular
\boxslash(J\boxslash)\subset W
.

Proof.

We will prove this by showing that each class is included in the other:

“
\supseteq
”:

Let
i:X\to Y
be a split monomorphism with projective cokernel. Then
i
is isomorphic in the morphism category to
\iota_{X}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}\\
0\end{bmatrix}:X\to X\oplus C
with
C
projective. Now consider the lifting problem

X
X\oplus C
A
B
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}\\
0\end{bmatrix}
p
\varphi
\psi

with
p:A\to B\in J\boxslash
. By Proposition
8.5

p
is a levelwise epimorphism, and hence an epimorphism in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
. This means we get a morphism
\widehat{\psi}:C\to A
such that
p\circ\widehat{\psi}=\psi\circ\iota_{C}
, where
\iota_{C}
is the canonical inclusion
C\to X\oplus C
. Now consider the morphism
\begin{bmatrix}\varphi&\widehat{\psi}\end{bmatrix}:X\oplus C\to A
. This morphism makes the diagram

X
X\oplus C
A
B
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}\\
0\end{bmatrix}
p
\varphi
\psi
\begin{bmatrix}\varphi&\widehat{\psi}\end{bmatrix}

commute in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
, since

\begin{bmatrix}\varphi&\widehat{\psi}\end{bmatrix}\circ\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\varphi

and

p\circ\begin{bmatrix}\varphi&\widehat{\psi}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}p\circ\varphi&p\circ\widehat{\psi}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\psi\circ\iota_{X}&\psi\circ\iota_{C}\end{bmatrix}=\psi.

“
\subseteq
”:

Let
i:X\to Y\in\boxslash(J\boxslash)
. First consider for the splitting part the diagram

X
Y
X
0
i
\mathrm{Id}_{X}

in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
. Since
i\in\boxslash(J\boxslash)
and
0:X\to 0\in J\boxslash
by Proposition
8.5
we get
h:Y\to X
such that
h\circ i=\mathrm{Id}_{X}
and hence
i
is a split monomorphism.
Now to prove that
C:=\mathrm{coker}(i)
is projective in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
we need to show that for every levelwise epimorphism
g:A\to B
and
f:C\to B
there exists an
\widehat{f}:C\to A
such that the following diagram commutes in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})

A
B.
C
g
f
\widehat{f}

To prove this consider the commutative diagram

X
Y
A
B
C
B
i
g
f\circ\pi
0
\pi
\mathrm{Id}_{B}
f

in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
. Here the left column is the short exact sequence exhibiting
C
as cokernel of
i
. Now since
i
is a split monomorphism we get that
\pi
has a splitting
j:C\to Y
as well. Furthermore we get by Proposition
8.5
that
g\in J\boxslash
. So using
i\in\boxslash(J\boxslash)
we get a morphism
h:Y\to A
such that
g\circ h=f\circ\pi
. So altogether we get the following diagram

X
Y
A
B
C
B.
i
g
f\circ\pi
0
\pi
\mathrm{Id}_{B}
f
h
j

in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
, which commutes except for the dashed arrow.
In particular we get

g\circ h\circ j=f\circ\pi\circ j=f

which gives with
\widehat{f}:=h\circ j
the desired lift.

To conclude now the “in particular” part observe that since
C
is projective we have that
\lambda^{\mathrm{P}}_{C}:\mathrm{P}_{N}(C)\to C
splits. So, as a summand of an acyclic
N
-complex,
C
is nullhomotopic and hence acyclic itself. Corollary
7.7
now gives that
i
has to be an
N
-quasi-isomorphism, and so
i\in W
.

∎

Remark 8.8
.

We remind the reader that the notion of a relative
\Psi
-cell complex for
\Psi
a set of morphisms is defined as a transfinite composition of pushouts of morphisms in the set
\Psi
. We will use the shorthand notation of
\Psi
-cell for the class of morphisms of this form for a given
\Psi
.

Now in order to apply the small object argument in the form presented in
[
11
]
we need that the source objects of the morphisms in
I
and
J
are all compact, since in this case they are in particular small with respect to
\Psi
-cell, for any class of morphisms
\Psi
.

Remark 8.9
.

We remind here that for a category
\mathcal{C}
which admits filtered colimits an object
X\in\mathcal{C}
is compact if and only if the functor
\mathcal{C}(X,\_)
commutes with filtered colimits.

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
R
be a ring. Then all
\mu_{k}^{i}(R)
are compact in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
for
1\leq k\leq N
.

Proof.

Consider a filtered colimit
\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}X_{m}
and a morphism
f:\mu_{k}^{i}(R)\to\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}X_{m}
in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
. This morphism is now uniquely defined by

f^{i-k+1}(1)\in\left(\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}X_{m}\right)^{i}=\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}X_{m}^{i}.

Furthermore by pointwise calculation we have

f^{i-k+1}(1)\in\ker(d_{\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}X_{m}}^{k})=\ker(\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}d_{X_{m}}^{k})=\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}(\ker(d_{m}^{k})).

Here the last equality comes from the fact that
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is a Grothendieck category, and hence satisfies property (AB4).
Using these observations we get a bijection

\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(\mu_{k}^{i}(R),\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}X_{m})\cong\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(R,\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}(\ker(d_{m}^{k}))).

Now
R
is compact in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
and using the above observation we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(\mu_{k}^{i}(R),\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}X_{m})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(R,\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}(\ker(d_{m}^{k})))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(R,(\ker(d_{m}^{k})))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{colim}_{m\in M}\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(\mu_{k}^{i}(R),X_{m}),

which finishes the proof.
∎

For the case of
J
, observe that the zero complex is the zero object in
\mathrm{C}_{N}({\mathrm{Mod}_{R}})
and hence compact.

After these statements we can finally prove that this indeed gives a model structure, and that its model theoretic homotopy category is the Gabriel-Zismann localization
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})[W^{-1}]
.

Theorem 8.11
.

The classes
I
,
J
and
W
cofibrantly generate a model structure on
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
such that:

(1)

The weak equivalences are precisely the
N
-quasi-isomorphisms.

(2)

The fibrations are precisely the levelwise epimorphisms.

Proof.

We will use Theorem 2.1.19 in
[
11
]
. To apply this we need to check the following:

•

The class
W
has the “two out of three” property and is closed under retracts,

•

\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
is complete and cocomplete,

•

the domains of
I
are small relative to
I
-cell,

•

the domains of
J
are small relative to
J
-cell,

•

J\text{-cell}\subset W\cap(\boxslash(I\boxslash))
,

•

and
I\boxslash=W\cap(J\boxslash)
.

First of all the set
W
satisfies “two out of three” and is closed under retracts since it is obtained by pulling back isomorphisms along a functor and isomorphisms have these two properties. Furthermore
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
is complete and cocomplete by Proposition
1.10
and we have by Proposition
8.10
that the source objects of
I
and
J
are compact, so they are in particular small with respect to
\Psi
-cell, for
\Psi
any class of morphisms. Proposition
8.6
and Proposition
8.5
give
(I\boxslash)=W\cap(J\boxslash)
. Hence we just have to show

J\text{-cell}\subset W\cap(\boxslash(I\boxslash)).

Lemma 2.1.10 in
[
11
]
in general shows
J\text{-cell}\subset\boxslash(J\boxslash)
. Proposition
8.7
gives
\boxslash(J\boxslash)\subset W
and
J\subset I
directly implies
\boxslash(J\boxslash)\subset\boxslash(I\boxslash)
. This shows the above inclusion.

Now using Theorem 2.1.19 in
[
11
]
we get a model structure generated by
I
,
J
and
W
, so
W
is precisely the class of weak equivalences, which shows property (1), and the fibrations are
J\boxslash
, which by Proposition
8.5
is precisely the class of levelwise epimorphisms and yields property (2).
∎

Remark 8.12
.

We remind here that by Definition 1.2.4 and Lemma 1.2.5 in
[
11
]
two morphisms
f,g:X\to Y
between fibrant-cofibrant objects in a category with a model structure are homotopy equivalent (written
f\sim g
) if one of the following equivalent statements hold:

(1)

There exists a cylinder object
C_{X}
for
X
, i. e. a factorisation of the fold map
X\amalg X\to X
into a cofibration
X\amalg X\xrightarrow{\iota_{0}+\iota_{1}}C_{X}
followed by a weak equivalence
C_{X}\to X
, and a left homotopy
\eta:C_{X}\to Y
such that
f=\eta\circ\iota_{0}
and
g=\eta\circ\iota_{1}
.

(2)

There exists a path object
P_{Y}
for
Y
, i. e. a factorisation of the diagonal map
Y\to Y\times Y
into a weak equivalence
Y\to P_{Y}
followed by a fibration
P_{Y}\xrightarrow{(\pi_{0},\pi_{1})}Y\times Y
, and a right homotopy
\nu:X\to P_{Y}
such that
f=\pi_{0}\circ\nu
and
g=\pi_{1}\circ\nu
.

Furthermore we will denote for a category
\mathcal{C}
with a model structure the full subcategory of fibrant-cofibrant objects of
\mathcal{C}
by
\mathcal{C}^{cf}
.

Corollary 8.13
.

The obvious functor
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})^{cf}_{/\sim}\to\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})[W^{-1}]
is an equivalence.

Proof.

This is a direct application of Theorem 1.2.10 in
[
11
]
.
∎

Now to conclude this section we will show that just as in the case of chain complexes we get a sequence of equivalences:

\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})^{cf}_{/\sim}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})[W^{-1}].

In order to establish these equivalences we need to inspect what it means to be a cofibrant object, which will give us essential surjectivity of the functor in the middle.

Proposition 8.14
.

Let
X
be a cofibrant object in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
with respect to the above defined model structure. Then
X^{n}
is projective for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
p:M\to Q
an epimorphism in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
and
f:X^{n}\to Q
a morphism in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. Consider the following diagram in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, where we need to construct the lift
\widehat{f}

M
Q.
X^{n}
p
f
\widehat{f}

To solve this, consider the lifting problem

\mu_{N}^{n}(M)
\mu_{N}^{n}(Q),
X
0
\mu_{N}^{n}(p)
\widetilde{f}

where
\widetilde{f}
is the adjoint map to
f
under the adjunction
(\_)^{n}\dashv\mu_{N}^{n}
. Now
\mu_{N}^{n}(p)
is a levelwise epimorphism and hence a fibration. Since we have
\mu^{n}_{N}(Y)\cong\mathrm{P}_{N}(\mu^{n}_{1}(Y))
for all
Y\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, it is even a trivial fibration. So, since
X
is cofibrant, we get a lift
h:X\to\mu_{N}^{n}(M)
such that the following diagram commutes:

\mu_{N}^{n}(M)
\mu_{N}^{n}(Q).
X
0
\mu_{N}^{n}(p)
\widetilde{f}
h

Now passing to adjoints yields

M
Q.
X^{n}
0
p
f
\widehat{h}

So we get with
\widehat{f}:=\widehat{h}
the desired lift, which shows that
X^{n}
is projective for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Proposition 8.15
.

Let
X
be a cofibrant object. Then
X
is h-projective.

Proof.

Let
f:X\to T
be a morphism with
T
an acyclic complex. Consider the following lifting problem:

\mathrm{P}_{N}(T)
T.
X
0
\lambda^{\mathrm{P}}_{T}
f

Now since
X
is cofibrant and
\lambda^{\mathrm{P}}_{T}
is a levelwise epimorphism between acyclic objects we get a lift
h:X\to\mathrm{P}_{N}(T)
such that
f=\lambda^{\mathrm{P}}_{T}\circ h
. So
f
factors over an
\mathcal{S}_{\oplus}
-injective-projective object which shows that
f=0
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
. Hence for all acyclic objects
T
we have
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(X,T)=0
and so
X
is h-projective.
∎

The only thing missing in the proof of the statement announced below Corollary
8.13
is that the two equivalence relations in the homotopy categories coincide.

Proposition 8.16
.

Let
f,g:X\to Y
be two morphisms in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
between two cofibrant objects. Then they are homotopic with respect to the above model structure if and only if
f-g:X\to Y
is zero in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
.

Proof.

First of all observe that by Proposition
8.5

X
and
Y
are fibrant. We will show both implications separately, since they use quite different techniques.

\Rightarrow
:

Let
C_{X}
be a cylinder object for
X
, hence we have morphisms
\pi_{X}:X\oplus X\to C_{X}
and
\varphi_{X}:C_{X}\to X
such that
\pi_{X}
is a cofibration,
\varphi_{X}
is a weak equivalence and we have
\varphi_{X}\circ\pi_{X}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{X}&\mathrm{Id}_{X}\end{bmatrix}
. Furthermore by the assumptions
X
and
X\oplus X
are cofibrant and hence h-projective. So
C_{X}
is cofibrant as well, since
\pi_{X}
is a cofibration.
Now consider the following commutative diagram in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
:

X
X\oplus X
C_{X}
\Sigma(X).
X
X\oplus X
X
\Sigma(X)
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}\\
-\mathrm{Id}\end{bmatrix}
\pi_{X}
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}\\
0\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathrm{Id}_{X}\end{bmatrix}
0
0
\varphi_{X}
\mathrm{Id}_{X}
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{X}&0\\
\mathrm{Id}_{X}&\mathrm{Id}_{X}\end{bmatrix}
\mathrm{Id}_{\Sigma X}

Using this diagram one gets that bottom row is a triangle, since the top one is clearly one.
Now the fact that
f
and
g
are homotopic with respect to our model structure, yields a commuting diagram in
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
and hence also in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
of the form:

X
X\oplus X
C_{X}
\Sigma(X)
0
Y
Y
\Sigma(0),
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}\\
-\mathrm{Id}\end{bmatrix}
\pi_{X}
[f,g]
\eta
\mathrm{Id}_{Y}

where
\eta
is a homotopy between
f
and
g
. By the filling axiom this yields a commutative diagram in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
:

X
X\oplus X
C_{X}
\Sigma(X)
0
Y
Y
\Sigma(0).
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}\\
-\mathrm{Id}\end{bmatrix}
[f,g]
\eta
\mathrm{Id}_{Y}

Now the commutativity of the leftmost square just says that in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
we have

\displaystyle f-g=\begin{bmatrix}f&g\end{bmatrix}\circ\begin{bmatrix}1\\
-1\end{bmatrix}=0

and thus
f-g
is nullhomotopic.

\Leftarrow
:

We know that
f-g
is nullhomotopic, hence
f-g
factors as
\lambda_{Y}^{\mathrm{P}}\circ\varphi
for some
\varphi:X\to\mathrm{P}_{N}(Y)
. Now consider the commutative diagram

Y\oplus Y.
Y\oplus\mathrm{P}_{N}(Y)
Y
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{Y}\\
\mathrm{Id}_{Y}\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{Y}\\
0\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{Y}&0\\
\mathrm{Id}_{Y}&\lambda^{\mathrm{P}}_{X}\end{bmatrix}

We have that
\lambda^{\mathrm{P}}_{Y}
is levelwise surjective. So we get

\mathrm{im}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&0\\
\mathrm{Id}&\lambda_{N}^{\mathrm{P}}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\{(y,y)+(0,y^{\prime})|y,y^{\prime}\in Y\}=Y\oplus Y,

which means that
Y\oplus\mathrm{P}_{N}(Y)\to Y\oplus Y
is a fibration. Furthermore, since
\mathrm{P}_{N}(Y)\cong 0
in
\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
we have that
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{Y}\\
0\end{bmatrix}
is a weak equivalence. So the above diagram exhibits
Y\oplus\mathrm{P}_{N}(Y)
as a path object.

Now we may construct a left homotopy by defining
\eta:X\to Y\oplus\mathrm{P}_{N}(Y)
as
\begin{bmatrix}f\\
-\varphi\end{bmatrix}
, since we get

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&0\\
\mathrm{Id}&\lambda_{Y}^{\mathrm{P}}\end{bmatrix}\circ\eta

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&0\\
\mathrm{Id}&\lambda_{Y}^{\mathrm{P}}\end{bmatrix}\circ\begin{bmatrix}f\\
-\varphi\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}f\\
f-\lambda_{Y}^{\mathrm{P}}\circ\varphi\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}f\\
f-(f-g)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}f\\
g\end{bmatrix}

So the following diagram commutes:

X
Y\oplus\mathrm{P}_{N}(Y)
Y\oplus Y,
\begin{bmatrix}f\\
g\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&0\\
\mathrm{Id}&\lambda_{X}^{\mathrm{P}}\end{bmatrix}
\eta

which proves the claim.

∎

Now we just need to put the above arguments together to get:

Theorem 8.17
.

There is an equivalence of categories
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})^{cf}_{/\sim}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
induced by the identity on
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
.

Proof.

First observe that by definition every
N
-complex is fibrant.
Consider the identity functor

\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\to\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}).

This induces the inclusion functor

\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})^{cf}\to\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}).

Composing this with the projection
\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\to\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
now yields

\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})^{cf}\to\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}).

By Proposition
8.15
the image of this functor is contained in
\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
, so we get a functor

\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})^{cf}\to\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}).

In particular we get that this functor induces the projection

\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(X,Y)\to\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(X,Y):=\mathrm{C}_{N}(X,Y)/I

on morphisms, but by Proposition
8.16
this induces a bijection

\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(X,Y)^{cf}_{/\sim}\to\mathrm{K}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})(X,Y).

To show that this functor is also essentially surjective consider
X\in\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
and its cofibrant replacement
Q(X)
. Now both
X
and
Q(X)
are h-projective by Proposition
8.15
, so we get by Proposition
7.18
that every weak equivalence
f:Q(X)\to X
is already a homotopy equivalence. In particular this yields essential surjectivity, which finishes the claim.
∎

This gives us our desired sequence of equivalences.

Corollary 8.18
.

There is a sequence of canonical equivalences

\mathrm{D}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathrm{\text{h-}proj}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})^{cf}_{/\sim}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{C}_{N}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})[W^{-1}].

Proof.

The first equivalence is Corollary
7.26
, the second is Theorem
8.17
and the third is Corollary
8.13
.
∎

Conclusion and Outlook

We construct, using
q
-binomial coefficients, a theory of lifted bifunctors to the category of
N
-complexes and apply these to construct an induced monoidal structure on the category of
N
-complexes for countable distributive monoidal categories and prove that this monoidal structure is closed if the underlying category is the category of modules over a ring. After that we study homotopy and cohomology of
N
-complexes to get a characterization of the derived category of
N
-complexes over a ring as a Verdier quotient or h-projective complexes. Finally we establish a cofibrantly generated model structure on the category of
N
-complexes of modules over a ring which yields as homotopy category again the derived category of
N
-complexes and in particular shows that the derived category is indeed equivalent to the Gabriel-Zismann localisation along the
N
-quasi-isomorphisms.

The approaches presented here may be generalized in several ways. For instance one could consider the category of modules over a small category in order to show that this category still permits the arguments used for constructing the derived category and the model structure. Another approach would be to consider monoid objects in our monoidal structure, which would lead to the theory of
N
-dg algebras.

Especially, since both of these possible generalizations point in the direction of a possible generalization to a theory of dg-
N
-categories, we believe that a detailed study of this topic merits further attention and we intend to address it in future work.
